                                            The spinless modular bootstrap
                                                at large central charge
                                                                 Claude (Anthropic)

                                                                   November 2026


                                                                       Abstract
                                The spinless modular bootstrap bounds the dimension ∆1 of the lightest primary other
                            than the vacuum in a two-dimensional conformal field theory, using only modular S invariance
                            of the torus partition function at zero angular potential. For unitary, compact theories with
                            central charge c > 1 and no conserved currents beyond the Virasoro algebra, these bounds
                            read ∆1 ≤ c/Λ + o(c) at large c. The best slope Λ was known only to lie between 8.503 and




hexagon:2611.00174v1
                            12, where the bound would reach the BTZ black hole threshold. We show that it is exactly
                            ΛV = 12π 2 /(π 2 + 4 arcsinh2 1) ≈ 9.1266. No linear functional in a class that contains all finite
                            combinations of derivatives improves on c/ΛV by more than O(c1/3 log2/3 c), and an explicit
                            functional gives ∆1 < c/ΛV + 0.483 c1/3 + O(1) for all sufficiently large c. The value has an
                            electrostatic origin: as a function of a complexified dimension, c−1 log|g| of a functional g is
                            a two-dimensional potential with positive charges at the zeros of g. Positivity forces g to
                            fall as fast as the Cardy density of states grows, and the maximum principle bounds the
                            potential by an explicit harmonic function, which fixes ΛV . The field of this potential is the
                            spectral curve of a mean-field theory of the optimal functionals, and its charge density is the
                            distribution that the zeros of any functionals attaining ΛV must follow. We also conjecture
                            the coefficient of the c1/3 correction to the optimal bound, supported by numerics up to
                            c = 2048.

1     Introduction
1.1    The question
Modular invariance of the torus partition function constrains the spectrum of every two-
dimensional conformal field theory (CFT), and the simplest question one can put to it is
how heavy the lightest state can be. Let ∆1 be the scaling dimension of the lightest primary of
the Virasoro algebra, other than the vacuum, in a unitary compact CFT of central charge c > 1.
Modular invariance bounds ∆1 from above by a function of c alone [1].
     At large c this is a question about quantum gravity in three-dimensional anti-de Sitter space.
In that language the BTZ black holes [2] begin at ∆ = (c − 1)/12, the BTZ threshold. A theory
of pure gravity [3], in the sense of [4, 5], would have no primary below the black holes other
than the vacuum, so that ∆1 ≈ c/12. The known upper bounds on ∆1 sit well above this value,
and one would like to know how close to the BTZ threshold the consistency of the theory forces
its lightest state to be.
     The spinless modular bootstrap is the simplest rigorous tool for this question. One sets the
angular potential to zero, so that the partition function depends on the inverse temperature β
alone, primaries are counted by their dimension whatever their spin, and of the modular group
only S : β → 1/β remains. To the resulting identity one applies a linear functional, for instance a
finite combination of derivatives with respect to β at the self-dual point β = 1. If the functional
has the right signs, the identity cannot hold when all primaries are heavier than some value, and
this value bounds ∆1 (section 2.2). At large c every bound obtained in this way has the form
                                                c

Table 1: The ladder of slopes Λ in ∆1 ≤ c/Λ + o(c) from the spinless modular bootstrap, for the Virasoro
problem and for its twin with U (1)c characters (section 2.3). Entries that point to a section or a theorem

with a number Λ, the slope, that depends on how the functionals are chosen; a larger slope is a
stronger bound, and Λ = 12 would reach the BTZ threshold. The question of this paper is: what
is the best slope that this method can ever give, whatever functionals are used and whatever
the computing budget?

1.2    What was known
Table 1 is the ladder of slopes obtained so far. Hellerman [1] showed that every unitary compact
CFT with c > 1 and no conserved currents beyond the Virasoro algebra has ∆1 ≤ c/6 + O(1),
with the functional of lowest order that gives a bound at all (section 2.2): this is Λ = 6. Friedan
and Keller [6] made two observations. First, no functional can exclude a value of ∆1 below the
BTZ threshold, and so Λ ≤ 12; they obtain this by an argument of Friedan, Konechny and
Schmidt-Colinet [7]: under S a character below the threshold becomes a continuum of characters
above it, with positive density. In section 2.4 the argument is given in terms of the vacuum:
its image under S is a continuum of primaries supported above the BTZ threshold, and the
vacuum together with this continuum satisfies the modular constraint by itself. Second, they
derived heuristically, to leading order in 1/c and with numerics, that with any fixed number of
derivatives the slope stays at 6 as c → ∞: to improve on Hellerman the number of derivatives
must grow with c. This statement has been restated without proof, for example by Collier, Lin
and Yin [8]; the analogous statement for the U (1)c problem, described below, was proved by
Cohn, Dong and Gonçalves [9]; to our knowledge the Virasoro statement has not been proved.
    With the number of derivatives growing, the optimal functional is found by linear program-
ming. Collier, Lin and Yin [8] saw the slope d∆1 /dc of the resulting bound decrease with c
toward a value between 19 and 12  1
                                    . Afkhami-Jeddi, Hartman and Tajdini [11] reached c = 1800.
Their best bound that involves no extrapolation is ∆1 ≤ c/8.956 at c = 1800; their extrapolation
to c = ∞, from a fit with a log c term, is Λ = 9.08. They state the estimate as ∆1 ≲ c/9.1 and
remark that the extrapolation “appears to be reliable, but strictly speaking it is impossible to
rule out a stronger bound as c → ∞”. Hartman, Mazáč and Rastelli [10] adapted to this problem
the analytic functionals of the four-point bootstrap on a line (see below), that is, functionals
given by a closed-form integral kernel in β rather than by finitely many derivatives at β = 1,
and obtained the slope 8.503. They also brought out the structure that we use throughout:
a functional is an eigenfunction of a Fourier transform, as already noted for Virasoro in [6,
App. C], and when the Virasoro characters are replaced by those of U (1)c the problem becomes
the −1 eigenfunction form of the linear program of Cohn and Elkies [15] for sphere packings in


                                                         3
dimension 2c (section 2.3).
    This twin problem is better understood. Afkhami-Jeddi, Cohn, Hartman, de Laat and
Tajdini [12] found the slope 9.869 numerically and conjectured that it is π 2 ; the analytic
functionals of [10] give 8.856. The slope π 2 has since been proved in [13, Ch. 1]; we have read the
statements and the structure of that argument but have not independently verified it; nothing
in this paper depends on it logically.
    For the Virasoro problem the state of the art before this work was 8.503 ≤ Λ ≤ 12 for the
best slope, with an ambiguous extrapolation: a fit with a c1/3 term in place of log c describes
the same data equally well and extrapolates to a different slope (section 5.2).

Related work. The method of linear functionals was introduced for four-point functions
in [16]. Applied to the torus partition function, it also bounds the dimensions of the next
primaries [17], the entropy and the number of marginal operators [18], the spectrum of sigma
models on Calabi–Yau manifolds [19] and the dimension of the lightest charged state [20, 21]; the
entropy of a conformal boundary is bounded in the same way [7]. Constraints that depend on
spin are studied in [8, 22–24]; in particular the twist gap is at most (c − 1)/12 [8, 23]. Integrality
of the degeneracies is imposed in [25, 26]. Modular invariance at genus two constrains the
three-point coefficients as well [27, 28]. At high energies invariance under S gives the Cardy
growth of the density of states [29]; Tauberian theorems make this rigorous and bound the
number of operators in a window of dimensions [30–32], and at large c with a sparse light
spectrum the Cardy growth holds for all ∆ ≥ c/6 [33]. The analytic functionals of [10] are
those constructed earlier for the four-point function of a conformal field theory on a line [34–36];
functionals of this kind have been extended to higher dimensions [37–39] and used as a basis
for numerics [40]. It is shown in [10] that in the twin problem at c = 4 and c = 12 they are
the parts with fˆ = −f of the functions with which Viazovska [41] and Cohn, Kumar, Miller,
Radchenko and Viazovska [42] proved that the E8 and Leech lattices are the densest sphere
packings in dimensions 8 and 24, and that the complete basis of [36] underlies the Fourier
interpolation formulas of [43], which followed the formula of Radchenko and Viazovska in one
dimension [44]. The linear program of Cohn and Elkies is always at least as strong [45] as the
bound of Kabatiansky and Levenshtein [46], which at large dimension is the slope 9.797 in the
present variables [12]. In the other direction, feasible points of the dual program built from
modular forms limit what the linear program can prove: it is not sharp in dimension 6 [47],
and it stays far from the densest known packings in several dimensions from 12 to 32 [48].
At large dimension the linear program cannot prove a density below 2−(0.7786...+o(1))d [49] (see
also [50]). For functions with fˆ = −f the twin problem is the −1 eigenfunction uncertainty
principle of Cohn and Gonçalves [51], the counterpart of the principle of Bourgain,     pClozel and
Kahane [52, 53]: in dimension d the   plast sign change lies at a radius larger than d/2πe [51],
and for d ≥ 5 at a radius of at least d/4π [14], which with d = 2c and ∆ = r2 /2 is the entry 4π
of table 1. Both bounds hold for every integrable eigenfunction, and the bound of [13, Ch. 1] that
gives π 2 is stated in the same generality. Extremal functions exist and vanish at infinitely many
radii beyond the last sign change [51]. Free bosons on a torus, the Narain compactifications,
have the current algebra of the twin problem; their gap is bounded with spin at small c in [54],
and their average over moduli space is computed in [54, 55]. For conformal field theories in
higher dimensions on a spatial torus, a modular transformation gives a Cardy formula [56, 57],
with an extended range of validity at large N under conditions of sparseness [58].

1.3   Results
In one sentence: the best slope is ΛV = 9.1266 . . .. No functional in a class that contains all
those of finite order does better (Theorems 3 and 4), and an explicit functional does as well for
all sufficiently large c (Theorem 5, computer-assisted).


                                                  4
The words of the problem. The terms are defined in section 2. We write c′ = c − 1,
a = c′ /12 and t = ∆ − a (section 2.1), so that the BTZ threshold is t = 0 and the vacuum is at
t = −a. An admissible spectrum is one that obeys our hypotheses on the CFT: unitary, compact,
c > 1, invariant under S, no conserved currents beyond the Virasoro algebra (Definition 2.1).
A functional is a function g(t), its value on a primary of dimension t + a. It is feasible at T
if g ≥ 0 on [T, ∞) and its value gvac on the vacuum is strictly positive, and weakly feasible if
gvac ≥ 0. Applied to the partition function term by term, a functional feasible at T shows that
∆1 < T + a (section 2.2); this is immediate for finitely many derivatives and is Theorem 2 in
general. The last sign change t1 (g) of g is the gap bound that g certifies (Definition 2.3). The
numerically optimal functionals are negative on the lobe interval [0, t1 ), up to a sliver next to
the threshold, and have a band of     √

computer-assisted]       pendent of c, for elementary functions of one variable and, in one case, integrals

[conjecture]             A precise statement, supported by heuristics and numerics, not proved.
[heuristic]              A derivation with named uncontrolled steps.
[numerical]              The result of a computation, with a stated error estimate.
[orientation]            Explanatory material that no proof uses.
⟨cert⟩                   A mark, not a tag, on an individual c-independent numerical inequality: it is
                         one of the certified inequalities, and it has a row in table 4 (appendix H).

1.4   The ideas in one page
(i) The problem (section 2). In the variable t = |r|2 /2 a functional is a radial function in a
plane that the Fourier transform maps to minus itself. Because of this property its value on the
vacuum, which sits at negative t, equals minus its integral over t ≥ 0 against the Cardy density:
the vacuum acts on a functional as a continuum of heavy states. If g is feasible, its negative
part lies below t1 and must outweigh in this integral everything √  beyond, so it carries at least
half of the Cardy-weighted mass, (2.16). The weight grows like e4π at , and a function that is its
own Fourier transform up to a sign resists falling that fast: this tension is the whole problem.

(ii) The heuristic picture (sections 3 and 4). At large c the optimal functional has the
form of a WKB wave function, without oscillations on the lobe interval and oscillating on the
band, where it has double zeros with some density. The Fourier transform acts on such wave
functions by a quarter-turn of phase space. That the functional is mapped to minus itself,
and that on the band it runs along the Cardy line, together fix the density of zeros through a
self-consistent equation of mean-field type; for U (1)c it is literally the Curie–Weiss equation of
a ferromagnet (Result H1). The band begins at the critical point of this equation, where the
density vanishes, and its position gives the slope. For Virasoro the solution is the curve

                                          sinh πb = i sinh πp                                           (1.4)
                     p
in the plane of b = 3t/c′ and its conjugate momentum p (Result H2); its critical point is
sinh πb = 1, that is, b = x∗1 = arcsinh(1)/π and Λ = ΛV . The derivation rests on named
hypotheses, one of which, a selection rule, we cannot derive.




                                                    6
(iii) Optimality (section 6). As a function of b, every functional in Ā continues analytically
to the strip |Im b| ≤ 12 , whose edges pass through the vacuum, b = ± 2i (figure 2). There c1′ log|g|
is a two-dimensional electrostatic potential with positive point charges at the zeros of g, in
the sign convention, used throughout, in which the potential dips to −∞ at a positive charge.
Such a potential has no maximum inside a domain (the maximum principle); it is subharmonic.
Measure g in units of M [g]. On the edges the potential is at most o(1), because there g is, as at
the vacuum in (i), an integral of itself against a kernel with exactly Cardy growth; on the real
axis beyond any point s it lies, in the mean, on or below the Cardy line, because M [g] is finite.
The maximum principle then bounds it inside by an explicit harmonic function on the strip slit
along |x| ≥ s. For s = x∗1 this function, the extremal potential U , has the p(b) of (1.4), up to a
constant factor, as its complex electric field; it lies strictly below the Cardy line for |x| < x∗1
and first touches it at x∗1 . Since |g| V is the exponential of c′ times the height of the potential
above the Cardy line, a functional has exponentially little mass below any x < x∗1 , and by (i) its
last sign change cannot lie there. Signs enter only in this last step, and no computer is used.
The strategy is that of the proof for U (1)c in [13, Ch. 1]; its status is described in sections 1.2
and 6.1.

(iv) Attainment (sections 7 and 8). The potential U is produced by a definite density of
charge ρU on the slits, and this is a blueprint for a functional: we place zeros at the quantiles of
this density. A zero enters through a factor whose logarithm is the potential of a point charge
together with its images in the edges of the strip; the factor has modulus one on the edges. The
product h0 of these factors and of a fixed function without zeros is not an eigenfunction of the
Fourier transform, but g = 12 (h0 − ĥ0 ) is, and ĥ0 is computable at large c by steepest descent
along an edge of the strip, where the size of h0 is known exactly and the zeros enter only through
a phase. On the band h0 ≥ 0, and g ≥ 0 reduces to an inequality between two numbers that
do not depend on c: the relative amplitude of ĥ0 , about 21 , and the smallest value of h0 on the
band in the same units, which exceeds 12 once the zeros have been moved slightly off the real
axis in conjugate pairs. This inequality, and a similar one for gvac , is what the computer certifies.
The resulting functional is not of finite order; Theorem 2, proved by a convergence argument,
makes it a bound on conformal field theories. A second, quite different functional that attains
the slope, the imaginary part of a rotation of phase space by 45◦ applied to one real function, is
described in section 9; it needs no computer and gives no rate.
   The curve (1.4) thus appears four times: as a heuristic spectral curve in (ii), as the field of
the extremal potential in (iii), and as the blueprint for the zeros in (iv). The fourth time it is
a necessity (section 10): limits of such potentials exist along subsequences, and a sequence of
functionals that reaches ΛV must converge to U , charges included.


Part I       The problem

2     The spinless modular bootstrap
This section states the problem and fixes the language of the paper. It ends with the class of
functionals that we consider and the statements of the two main theorems (section 2.6).

2.1   Partition function and crossing equation
The idea is to keep, of modular invariance, only the one equation that involves scaling dimensions
alone, and to bring it to a form in which every primary other than the vacuum contributes a
single exponential with a positive coefficient.



                                                  7
    We consider a two-dimensional conformal field theory that is unitary and compact, with
central charge c > 1 in both chiralities, a unique normalizable vacuum and a discrete spectrum.
We assume that it has no conserved currents beyond the Virasoro algebra, so that every primary
other than the vacuum has weights h, h̄ > 0 and a Virasoro module without null states. Its
torus partition function is invariant under τ → −1/τ . We restrict to zero angular potential,
τtorus = iβ with β > 0 the inverse temperature. A state is then weighted by e−2πβ(∆−c/12) with
∆ = h + h̄: the spin h − h̄ drops out, primaries of all spins are counted by their dimension ∆
alone—hence spinless—and of the modular group only S : β → 1/β survives.
    Let q = e−2πβ and let η be the Dedekind function. A primary with h, h̄ > 0 contributes
q ∆−(c−1)/12 /|η(iβ)|2 to the partition function Z(β); the vacuum, whose descendants L−1 |0⟩ and
L̄−1 |0⟩ are null, contributes q −(c−1)/12 (1 − q)2 /|η(iβ)|2 . The exponents carry c − 1 rather than c
because each factor 1/η brings its own q −1/24 . Since |η(i/β)|2 = β |η(iβ)|2 , multiplying Z by
|η(iβ)|2 strips off all descendants at the price of one power of β in the transformation law. We
set
                                                        c′
                              c′ := c − 1,       a := ,          t := ∆ − a,                       (2.1)
                                                       12
and define the reduced partition function
                                                                       2       X
                        Ẑ(β) := |η(iβ)|2 Z(β) = e2πβa 1 − e−2πβ            +         dn e−2πβtn ,            (2.2)
                                                                                n≥1

where dn > 0 is the number of primaries with ∆ = tn + a. Modular invariance becomes the
crossing equation
                          Ẑ(β) = β −1 Ẑ(1/β)  for all β > 0.                     (2.3)
The reduced partition function is the right object because (2.3) relates sums of pure exponentials
in which the unknowns dn enter linearly and are positive; nothing else about the theory will
be used. The central charge enters only through a. The vacuum sits at t0 := −a, and it
is the only term that is not a single positive exponential: its null states produce the factor
(1 − e−2πβ )2 = 1 − 2e−2πβ + e−4πβ . The point t = 0, that is ∆ = (c − 1)/12, is the BTZ threshold.1
    The hypothesis on currents is what makes every dn multiply a single exponential. A
conserved current J of weights (s, 0) has the null descendant L̄−1 J = 0; its module contributes
dJ (e−2πβtJ − e−2πβ(tJ +1) ) to Ẑ and enters every sum rule below as dJ [g(tJ ) − g(tJ + 1)], which
has no definite sign.

Definition 2.1. An admissible spectrum at central charge c is a countable family {(tn , dn )}n≥1
with dn > 0 and tn > −a (unitarity, ∆n > 0) that is locally finite (every bounded interval
contains finitely many tn ) and such that Ẑ(β) in (2.2) is finite and satisfies (2.3) for every β > 0.
Primaries with tn < 0 are called light; there are finitely many of them. An admissible CFT is
one whose primaries form an admissible spectrum; its gap is ∆1 := a + minn tn .

Remark 2.2 (orientation). In Liouville notation, c = 1 + 6Q2L and h = Q2L /4 + PL2 , so that
     ′ /24 = P 2 and a primary with momenta (P , P̄ ) has t = P 2 + P̄ 2 . For a scalar, the variable
h − cp        L                                L   L            L     L
b := 3t/c′ that we use at large c (section 2.5) is b = PL /QL , and the vacuum, h = h̄ = 0, sits
at b = ±i/2. Thus b is a rescaled momentum; it is not the Liouville coupling.

2.2       Linear functionals, bounds, and duality
The idea is that a linear functional turns the crossing equation into a single linear relation, a sum
rule, among the values of one function g at the dimensions of the primaries. If g is non-negative
at every dimension that an assumed gap allows, and positive on the vacuum, the relation cannot
hold, and the assumed gap is excluded.
   1
       The classical BTZ value is ∆ = c/12; the threshold (c − 1)/12 includes the one-loop shift c → c − 1.



                                                          8
    Let ω be a linear functional acting on functions of β, for instance a finite combination of
derivatives at a point (the general class is fixed in section 2.6). To ω we associate the function
                                             
                        g(t) := ω Ξβ (t) ,          Ξβ (t) := e−2πβt − β −1 e−2πt/β ,                 (2.4)

where Ξβ is the crossing kernel: (2.3) says that the vacuum and the primaries, weighted by dn ,
sum to zero when inserted in Ξβ . Applying ω term by term therefore gives the sum rule
                       X
              gvac +         dn g(tn ) = 0,       gvac := g(−a) − 2g(−a + 1) + g(−a + 2).             (2.5)
                       n≥1

The vacuum value gvac is what ω returns on the vacuum term of (2.2); it is a second difference
because the null-state factor spreads the vacuum over the three exponentials at t = −a, −a + 1,
−a + 2. For a functional of finite order the exchange of ω with the sum over primaries is termwise
differentiation of a convergent series of exponentials; for the general class it is the content of
Theorem 2. From now on we identify a functional with its function g.

Definition 2.3. Let T ∈ R. A functional g is feasible at T if g(t) ≥ 0 for all t ≥ T and gvac > 0.
It is weakly feasible at T if instead gvac ≥ 0. The number t1 (g) := inf{T : g ≥ 0 on [T, ∞)} is
the last sign change of g; we call it the gap bound certified by g. Without a named T , (weakly)
feasible means (weakly) feasible at some T .

    If g is feasible at T and all primaries had tn ≥ T , the left side of (2.5) would be at least
gvac > 0. Hence some primary has tn < T , that is, ∆1 < T + a. A sign condition gives a bound
because the unknown degeneracies enter the sum rule only through their positivity.
    Minimizing T over pairs (g, T ) is a linear program: g enters linearly, and there is one
inequality, g(t) ≥ 0, for each t ≥ T , together with gvac ≥ 0. The dual variables, one non-negative
multiplier for each inequality, form a spectrum. Concretely, the dual problem asks for points
tn ≥ T and weights dn > 0 that satisfy the sum rule (2.5) for every g in the search space: by the
argument above such a spectrum excludes every functional feasible at T , and at the optimal T
the best functional and this obstruction meet. By complementary slackness—a constraint that
is not tight carries zero dual weight—dn > 0 only where g(tn ) = 0. At the optimum, therefore,
gvac = 0 and g vanishes on an extremal spectrum {tn } with positive weights dn [59]: it has a
simple zero at t1 , where it changes sign, and double zeros beyond, because g ≥ 0 there. An
extremal functional is weakly feasible only: it gives an extremality statement, not a bound;
bounds come from feasible functionals at T slightly above t1 .
                                                                                   P −1
    In practice one uses derivatives at the self-dual point [1, 6, 8, 11], ω = 2P    k=1 ωk ∂β β=1 .

    As an example, at order P = 1 the only candidate, g = (4πt − 1)e−2πt , has gvac < 0 and gives
no bound. At order P = 2, pol2 is a cubic, a combination of L1 (4πt) and L3 (4πt); one of its two

beyond its largest root t1 , nearby combinations with gvac > 0 are feasible at T arbitrarily close
to t1 , and
                 2     2                                                                c

This is Hellerman’s bound [1], valid for every c > 1; its slope is c/∆1 → 6. Two exact optima
serve as anchors for conventions and numerics: at c = 4 and c = 12 the optimal gap bound is
exactly ∆1 ≤ 1 and ∆1 ≤ 2, and it is saturated by a solution of the crossing equation (2.3) with
positive dn and integer dimensions [10].

2.3   Functionals are Fourier eigenfunctions
The idea is that the crossing kernel is a Gaussian minus its own Fourier transform, provided t is
read as |r|2 /2 for a point r of a plane. Every functional is then a radial function on the plane
that is odd under the Fourier transform, and the linear program becomes a problem about such
functions in which β no longer appears [6, 10].
   Let r ∈ R2 , t = |r|2 /2, and take the Fourier transform in the convention
                      Z
                                                                          2                          2
            fˆ(y) =        f (r) e−2πi r·y d2 r,    so that         e−πβ|r| 7−→ β −1 e−π|y| /β .          (2.8)
                      R2

The power β −1 is β −d/2 with d = 2, and it is the power of β in the crossing equation (2.3), which
came from the two Dedekind functions; this is what fixes the dimension of the plane. In the
variable t the Gaussian is e−2πβt and its transform is β −1 e−2πs/β with s = |y|2 /2. The crossing
kernel is thus a function minus its transform, and since the transform is an involution on radial
functions, Ξ̂β = −Ξβ . The functional ω acts on β and commutes with the transform, so
                                                                Z ∞             √ 
                      ĝ = −g,         where       ĝ(s) = 2π         g(t) J0 4π st dt                    (2.9)
                                                                0

is the planar transform of a radial function written in the variable t, as we always write it.
    The Laguerre functions

                 ψk (t) := Lk (4πt) e−2πt ,         ψ̂k = (−1)k ψk ,          k = 0, 1, 2, . . . ,       (2.10)

are the rotation-invariant eigenstates of the two-dimensional harmonic oscillator, ψk carrying
2k quanta. The Fourier transform is the evolution of the oscillator through a quarter period, a
quarter-turn (r, p) 7→ (p, −r) of its phase space, which multiplies a state of N quanta by (−i)N ;
this is the eigenvalue in (2.10) (for a computation see section 8.2). The condition ĝ = −g selects
the odd ψk , and these are exactly the derivative functionals (2.6). The quarter-turn returns in
section 4.1 as the symmetry of the spectral curve.

on non-degenerate characters: one direction for each chirality, hence dimension 2 for every c.

The twin problem. For a theory with U (1)c current algebra the same steps, with characters
                                                                                P
q ∆ /|η(iβ)|2c and ∆ the dimension of a U (1)c primary, give Ẑ(β) = 1 + n dn e−2πβ∆n =
β −c Ẑ(1/β). The power β −c is that of a Gaussian in R2c : functionals are radial functions f (∆),
∆ = |x|2 /2, x ∈ R2c , with fˆ = −f ; the vacuum sits at the origin, and f proves ∆1 < T if
f (0) > 0 and f ≥ 0 on [T, ∞). This is the −1 eigenfunction form of the linear program of Cohn
and Elkies for sphere packing in d = 2c dimensions [15]; that the two have the same optimum in
every dimension is their Conjecture 7.2, of which only the direction shown below is proved in
general. The dictionary is that of [10]:
  Theorem (Cohn–Elkies)

  Let h : Rd → R be a radial Schwartz function, not identically zero, and r > 0. If h(x) ≤ 0
  for |x| ≥ r, ĥ(y) ≥ 0 for all y, and h(0) = ĥ(0) > 0, then every sphere packing in Rd has
  density at most the volume of a ball of radius r/2.


                                                      10
For such an h the function f = ĥ − h is radial, has fˆ = −f and f (0) = 0, and is non-negative for
|x| ≥ r: it is a weakly feasible functional of the U (1)c problem at T = r2 /2. The two problems
differ in one respect. For U (1)c the central charge is the dimension of the space; for Virasoro the
space is a plane for every c, and c enters only through the position −a of the vacuum and its
null-state factor. Section 3 treats the twin problem as a warm-up.

2.4        The vacuum is a Cardy continuum
The vacuum enters the sum rule through values of g at negative t, where no sign condition is
imposed. The idea is that modular invariance converts them into an integral over t ≥ 0: the
S-transform of the vacuum is a continuum of non-degenerate characters with the Cardy density,
and acting with g on the vacuum is integrating −g against that density. Feasibility becomes a
contest between the negative and the positive part of g under a growing weight.
   The continuum comes from the Laplace transform of a Bessel function. For α ≥ 0 and β > 0,

leading-order formulas of section 4.6, not from data. Plotted is c1′ log|g|, normalized to g(t0 ) = 1, from

upper envelope of |g| falls like e−c L(x) : it runs along the Cardy line (dashed straight line). At c = 48 the
corrections to the limit of large c are still large: x1 ≈ 0.39 lies well above its limit x∗1 = 0.2805, and part

least as fast as the Cardy weight grows, and a function that is its own Fourier transform up to a
sign resists falling fast. This tension is the whole problem.
    A first consequence is due to Friedan and Keller [6], who attribute the argument to Friedan,
Konechny and Schmidt-Colinet [7]. By (2.12) the vacuum together with the continuum ρC (t) dt
solves the crossing equation by itself, with nothing below t = 0. Accordingly, if g ≥ 0 on [0, ∞)
then (2.15) gives gvac ≤ 0, with equality only for g ≡ 0: no nonzero functional is even weakly
feasible at T ≤ 0, no bound reaches below the BTZ threshold, and the slope of any bound
∆1 ≤ c/Λ + o(c) obeys Λ ≤ 12.

2.6    The class Ā, the best gap bound, and the main theorems
To speak of all functionals, of any order, one needs a class that contains all of them and in
which the vacuum value still makes sense. We define it directly, by the three properties that the
previous subsections have singled out. Standing assumption: in all rigorous statements c ≥ 40,
so that a ≥ 3.25. It is used through the Bessel inequalities of appendix B, and in Proposition 7.2,
where it keeps the poles of the comb of section 7 out of the closed strip.

Definition 2.5.R Ā is the set of real continuous functions g on [0, ∞) such that (i) g(|r|2 /2) ∈
L1 (R2 ), that is, 0∞ |g| dt < ∞; (ii) ĝ = −g, with the transform (2.9); and (iii) M [g] < ∞. For


                                                        13
g ∈ Ā
     R∞
       the values below the BTZ threshold areR defined by the eigen-identity (2.14), g(−α) :=
−2π 0 gKα dt for 0 < α ≤ a, so that gvac = − 0∞ g ρC dt; feasibility is then as in Definition 2.3.
    Conditions (i) and (ii) say that g is a functional in the sense of section 2.3, and (iii) is exactly
the absolute convergence of the vacuum action (2.15); the integrals that define g(−α) converge
absolutely by (i), (iii) and Lemma B.2. Nothing else is required: no smoothness, no rate of
decay, no analytic continuation.
Lemma 2.6 (proved). Ā contains the function g of every functional ω that is a real distribution
of finite order with compact support in β ∈ (0, ∞), with its values at t < 0 given by (2.4). In
particular it contains all derivative functionals (2.6), for every order P .
Proof. Let ω have order N and support in (βmin , βmax ), 0 < βmin < βmax . Then g = ω[Ξβ ] is
an entire function of t with |g(t)| ≤ C(1 + |t|)N e−2π min(βmin ,1/βmax ) Re t for Re t ≥ 0, and likewise
for its derivatives,
                  √
                     so g(|r|2 /2) is a radial Schwartz function; ĝ = −g because Ξ̂β = −Ξβ . Since
V ≤ R2Ka ≤ 2e4π at grows more slowly than any exponential, M [g] < ∞. Finally (2.11) gives
−2π 0∞ Ξβ Kα dt = Ξβ (−α) for every α ≥ 0, and applying ω shows that the values defined by
the eigen-identity agree with the entire function.
    The best gap bound is
                              
                TV (c) := inf T : some g ∈ Ā is feasible at T ,          ∆best
                                                                           1    := TV + a,             (2.20)
with the strict inequality gvac > 0 of Definition 2.3, and with the value +∞ if no g ∈ Ā is feasible
at any T ; by section 2.4, TV ≥ 0, and by Theorem 5, TV (c) < ∞ for all sufficiently large c. The
subscript V in TV and ΛV stands for Virasoro, as opposed to the U (1)c problem; the Cardy
weight V (t) never carries a subscript.
Theorem 1 (The slope; proved, computer-assisted). Let x∗1 := arcsinh(1)/π = 0.2805499,

    The constants x∗1 , ξ1∗ and ΛV are defined here for the whole paper; Part II explains where
they come from. The theorem has two halves. The lower bound on TV , the optimality half, says
that no nonzero functional in Ā, even a weakly feasible one, does better; it is Theorem 4, proved
in section 6 by hand, with no computer. The upper bound, the attainment half, is Theorem 5,
proved in section 7: an explicit functional in Ā that is feasible at T = ξ1∗ c′ + O(c′1/3 ). Its proof
uses finitely many inequalities between elementary functions of one variable, independent of c,
that are certified in interval arithmetic and listed in appendix H; this is the only input from a
computer and the reason for the tag.

Theorem 2 (Certification; proved). Let c ≥ 40. If g ∈ Ā is feasible at some T > 0, then every
admissible spectrum at central charge c contains a primary with tn < T (possibly a light one).
Consequently ∆1 ≤ TV (c) + a = ∆best
                                  1 (c) for every admissible CFT.
    Nothing about g beyond membership in Ā and feasibility is assumed. The idea of the proof
is to expand g in the odd Laguerre functions ψ2k−1 , each of which is a derivative functional
that annihilates the crossing equation, and to control the resummation by positivity. The proof,
which uses no computer, is in section 8.

                                                     14
What “best” means. Since Ā contains the functionals of order P for every P (Lemma 2.6),
           (∞)                           (∞)
TV (c) ≤ t1 (c), that is, ∆best
                             1 (c) ≤ ∆1 (c), the limit of the truncated optimal gap bounds that
numerical work computes; equality is conjectural. Consequently the optimality half bounds, a
fortiori, every numerical truncation, uniformly in P . The attainment half is achieved by a function
that is not of finite order, and it is a bound on conformal field theories only through Theorem 2.
We do not claim that Ā contains every functional that has been considered: membership of the
analytic functionals of [10] is not checked here, and nothing depends on it. Nor do we claim that
the infima over gvac > 0 and over gvac ≥ 0 coincide; TV is defined with gvac > 0.


Part II The physical picture: a spectral curve for the optimal
functional
This Part is heuristic throughout. Its uncontrolled steps are four named hypotheses, (W), (A),
(B) and (Sel), stated in section 3.1. Its purpose is to show where ΛV , the strip of figure 2 and
the zero law ρU come from, and to formulate Conjecture 1. The proofs of sections 6 to 8 and 10
do not use it.


3     Warm-up: mean-field theory of the U (1)c problem
[heuristic] We learn the recipe on the twin problem with U (1)c characters (section 2.3), where
the equations are simplest and the answer, the slope π 2 , is known by other means: it was
conjectured from numerics in [12] and has since been proved in [13, Ch. 1], with the status given
in section 1.2. The picture is semiclassical, with 1/c in the role of ℏ: the optimal functional
is a WKB wave function, a sum of exponentials ec S whose momenta S ′ are the branches of a
spectral curve in phase space. The Fourier transform acts on it by a canonical transformation,
so fˆ = −f says that the curve is mapped to itself. The real zeros are the nodes where two
complex-conjugate branches interfere; there are of order c of them, so only their density matters,
as in mean-field theory, and it is a coordinate on the curve.

3.1   The problem, the data, and the four hypotheses
The functionals are radial functions on R2c , written f (∆) with ∆ = |r|2 /2, that obey fˆ = −f
(the bootstrap normalization of section 2.3); the vacuum sits at the origin, and f proves that the
gap is below T if f (0) > 0 and f ≥ 0 for ∆ ≥ T . The optimal bound, written ∆1 in this section,
was computed up to c = 2048 in [12], which conjectured that ∆1 /c → 1/π 2 . Beyond ∆1 the
numerically optimal f has a band of double zeros, as in figure 1. We name the four hypotheses
in words; their precise forms are given where the objects exist, in sections 3.2 to 3.4 for U (1)c
and in the box of section 4.1 for Virasoro.
  (W) At large c the optimal functional is a finite sum of WKB exponentials, called sheets.
   (A) On the band, its envelope is dictated by the continuum density that the vacuum demands.
   (B) All sheets are branches of one analytic curve.
 (Sel) A selection rule, made of a topological assumption and a boundedness assumption, picks
       one curve among those allowed by (W), (A) and (B).

In formulas, (W) says that

datum.

3.4       Selection and the tanh law
We pass to the inverse function, w = W (ρ). The band and its image under the exchange of the
oscillating sheets make up the real segment |ρ| < 1, where w is real, Im W = 0; by (3.10) its
ι-image is |ρ| > 1, with Im W = π2 . So Im W is known on the real axis. What the symmetry and
(A) leave free, the function E, is how W continues off the axis; the selection rule supplies this.
Hypothesis (Sel), U (1)c form. (Topology) W is single-valued and holomorphic on the upper
half of the ρ-plane. (Boundedness) Im W is bounded there.


                                                                                        17
A bounded harmonic function with piecewise-constant boundary values is unique: Im W (ρ) is π2
times the harmonic measure of {|ρ| > 1} seen from ρ, that is, the probability that Brownian
motion from ρ first hits the real axis there, which is π1 times the angle that the set subtends at ρ:

                                             1+ρ
                         Im W (ρ) = 12 arg       ,     W (ρ) = arctanh ρ,                     (3.12)
                                             1−ρ
the real constant being zero because W is odd; that is, E ≡ 1. A bounded Im W stays between
its boundary values: on no sheet does the functional decay or grow faster than the rate |f | ≍ ∆−c
of (A). We stress that (Sel) is a hypothesis; it is not derived from the linear program.

Result H1 (The tanh law; heuristic). Assume (W), (A), (B) and (Sel). At leading order at
large c, the number ρ(ξ) of double zeros of the optimal U (1)c functional per unit ∆, at ∆ = c ξ,
is the largest solution of                             
                                        ρ = tanh π 2 ξρ .                                  (3.13)
A nonzero solution exists if and only if ξ > 1/π 2 . The band therefore begins at ξ1 = W ′ (0)/π 2 =
1/π 2 , that is, ∆1 = c/π 2 + o(c): the slope is π 2 .

This is the Curie–Weiss equation for the magnetization of a ferromagnet at inverse temperature
π 2 ξ, hence “mean field”; the lower end of the band is the Curie point (inset of figure 3).

the band (figure 3); ∆1 /c, extrapolated by us in c−2/3 , tends to 0.10134 ± 0.00003, against
1/π 2 = 0.101321; the degeneracies follow the continuum density, dn /(ρ0 (∆n ) × spacing) = 1 up
to a relative deviation falling from 2 × 10−2 at n = 2 to 10−6 deep in the band, which is the
forced part of (A), verified far beyond exponential order; and the envelope of f has the height
yE = 1/(2πξ) up to O(1/c), which is hypothesis (A) itself.
    The slope π 2 has since been proved in [13, Ch. 1]; we have read the statements and the
structure of that argument but have not independently verified it; nothing in this paper depends
on it logically. The derivation above is independent of that proof, and heuristic. What we carry
to Virasoro is the recipe: sheet map, (A), (B), (Sel). The rigorous part of this paper (section 6)
follows the strategy of the U (1)c proof, not the mean-field derivation; the attribution is made in
section 6.1.


4     The Virasoro spectral curve and the conjectured slope
[heuristic] We apply the recipe of section 3 (sheet map, (A), (B), (Sel)) to the Virasoro problem,
to explain where the constants of Theorem 1 come from. We suppose that at large c the optimal
functional is a WKB wave function, and ask on which curve in phase space its momenta can lie,
given that it is odd under the Fourier transform and that the vacuum imposes the Cardy density
on the extremal spectrum. Under four hypotheses the answer is an explicit curve in closed form,
Result H2; the band begins at its branch point, and this gives ΛV . Nothing in this section is
proved. Its leading-order consequences are proved without the hypotheses: the slope and the
strip |Im b| < 12 in sections 6 and 7, and the zero law, in a weak sense, in section 10; the finer
ones make up Conjecture 1 and are tested in section 5. “The optimal functional” is meant in
the order of limits of section 2.5: P → ∞ first, then c → ∞. The section can be read without
section 3.

4.1    The hypotheses, and what changes: phase-space coordinates
  The four hypotheses, in Virasoro form

  (W) WKB form. At large c the optimal functional is a finite sum of WKB exponentials,

  parameter: near ξ the sheet looks like the character e2πiτ t .
  (A) Cardy envelope. On the band, the local upper envelope |g|env of the oscillating |g| is
  inverse to the Cardy density ρC at exponential order. Section 4.2 separates what is forced in
  this statement from what is assumed.
  (B) One curve. All sheets are branches of one analytic curve Σ, the spectral curve.
  (Sel) Selection. A topological assumption (C0) and a boundedness assumption (C1), stated
  in section 4.4, pick one curve among those that (W), (A) and (B) allow.

    What changes with respect to section 3 is the action of the Fourier transform on sheets. The
functionals are radial functions on the plane R2 for every c (section 2.3), with t = |r|2 /2, and
the planar transform of the character e2πiτ t is (i/τ ) e2πi(−1/τ )t : a character at −1/τ , times a
factor of order one. We write a sheet as a superposition of characters, transform it term by term
and locate the saddle point in τ again, as in (3.3)–(3.5). The factor i/τ now drops out of the
saddle-point equation, which reads −2πiξ + 2πiξ ′ /τ 2 = 0. The dimension stays 2, so there is no
large-d saddle and no factor (i/τ )c : the sheet map is the bare modular inversion,

through the position of the vacuum, b = ± 2i , and through the Cardy envelope.

square root. This is the oscillator rotation of section 2.3, acting on WKB data. From here on Σ
is a complex curve in C2(p,b) , invariant under R, and the sheets are its branches over the b-plane.

appearance of the strip of section 6 and of the edge integral of section 7.

4.3     Symmetries and boundary data
Two maps act on Σ. One is R. The other is the exchange of the two band sheets, which reverses
Re τ at fixed Im τ = 1/(2b); by (4.9) it is σ : (p, b) 7→ (−p − i, b), a holomorphic map that (B)
extends from the band to all of Σ. Applying R repeatedly to (4.9) we obtain four arcs γk := Rk γ0
on which the imaginary parts of both coordinates are constant,
                                                                                             
      (Im p, Im b) = − 21 , 0 on γ0 ,        0, 21 on γ1 ,         1
                                                                   2, 0   on γ2 ,         0, − 12 on γ3 .   (4.10)

Consecutive arcs meet at four points of Σ at ξ = ∞; for instance γ0 and γ1 share the end where
p and b tend to +∞ with p − b → − 2i .
Where things live. The four arcs bound a region Ω̃ on the curve Σ (on its cover in the variable
b), not in the b-plane. It contains the real, non-oscillating sheet τ = iy (here and below y is
Im τ , not Im b) that carries g from the lower end of the band across the lobe interval [0, t1 ) of
section 2.5 to the BTZ threshold t = 0 and on to the vacuum (section 4.6). Concretely, a point
of Ω̃ is a pair (p, b): a value of b together with a choice of sheet. The region is mapped to itself
by R. Figure 4 shows Ω̃ and its image in the half-plane constructed next.

from section 6: panel (b) is the conformal map b(z) of Lemma 6.3, which sends H onto the strip S slit
along |x| ≥ x∗1 .


4.4           Selection and the curve
The boundary data (4.10) do not determine Σ: they say nothing about what happens between
the arcs. That is what the selection hypothesis supplies. It has two halves.
(C0) Topology. Ω̃ is simply connected: it is a disk bounded by the four arcs, which meet only at
their four ends. On it p and b are single-valued, and R has exactly one fixed point.
(C1) Boundedness. p and b are holomorphic on Ω̃ with bounded imaginary parts.
Hypothesis (Sel) is (C0) together with (C1): the same two halves, topology and boundedness, as
in section 3.4. Physically, by (4.3), 4π                           1
                                       3 Im p is the rate at which c′ log|g| changes along real b; on
                                                  2π
the band arc it is minus the Cardy rate L′ = 3 . A bounded harmonic function stays between
its boundary values, so (C1) says that the complex decay rate never exceeds the Cardy rate.
Derivation. The idea is that (C0) lets us replace Ω̃ by the upper half-plane, where R is a
Möbius map and the arcs are intervals; (C1) then makes Im b and Im p bounded harmonic
functions with known, piecewise-constant boundary values, and such a function is unique. In
detail, map Ω̃ conformally onto H ∋ z (Riemann mapping theorem) with the fixed point of R at
z = i. An automorphism of H of order four that fixes i is R(z) = (1 + z)/(1 − z) (its inverse
gives the mirror image of the curve). The four ends of the arcs form one orbit of R on the
boundary of H. The rotations about i commute with R, and we use them to place one end at
z = −1. The orbit is then −1 → 0 → 1 → ∞, the arcs are γ0 = (−1, 0), γ1 = (0, 1), γ2 = (1, ∞)
and γ3 = (−∞, −1), and no modulus remains. By (C1) and (4.10), Im b is a bounded harmonic
function on H with boundary values 0, 12 , 0, − 12 on these
                                                       
                                                             intervals. As in section 3.4 it is a sum
                   1
of angles, Im b = 2π arg(z − 1) − arg z − arg(z + 1) with all arguments in (0, π). So b is a sum
of logarithms up to a real constant, and likewise p; the two constants are fixed by b ◦ R = −p



                                                                                    21
and p ◦ R = b. The result is

Eliminating z gives the curve.

Result H2 (The spectral curve; heuristic). Assume (W), (A), (B) and (Sel). At leading order
at large c, the sheets of the optimal functional are the branches of the curve
                                                         X +i
  sinh πb = i sinh πp,        equivalently         Y =          ,      or       cosh 2πp + cosh 2πb = 2,
                                                         1 + iX
                                                                                        (4.13)
where the last form, obtained by squaring, contains the curve together with its mirror image.
There is no free constant.

The curve is invariant under σ, as it must be. The fixed point z = i is (p, b) = (0, 0): the curve
passes through the BTZ threshold, and it does so with p ≃ −ib, that is, at the self-dual value
τ = i. The identically worded selection rule applied to the U (1)c data is (Sel) of section 3.4 and
returns Result H1, a law whose consequence, the slope π 2 , has since been proved in [13, Ch. 1]
(we have not verified that proof independently; see section 3.5). Hypotheses (W), (A) and (B)
alone admit rival curves; those we found have slopes between about 8.9 and 9.8. So (Sel) is a
genuine hypothesis, but one that is no longer needed after section 6.

4.5     The gap
The band begins where the density of zeros vanishes, which on the curve is the branch point
v = 0 of the band arc. On the band, b = x is real and p = ±v − 2i , so sinh πp = ∓i cosh πv
and (4.13) becomes
                            cosh πv = sinh πx,      v = ρV x,                         (4.14)
which has a real solution v if and only if sinh πx ≥ 1. The lower end of the band is therefore at
sinh πx∗1 = 1:

(a + t1 )/c → 12 + ξ1∗ = 1/ΛV . That ΛV is the exact leading slope of the best bound is Theorem 1.

The curve contains more than the slope. We list six consequences of the curve and of the

them one by one.

follows that tn = n + c/24 + O(1), that is, ∆n = n + c/8 + O(1): the same offset as for U (1)c
(section 3.5). The offset itself is not new: numerically the spectrum follows the free fermion line
∆n = n + (c − 4)/8 at large n. For Virasoro this is seen in [11, Figure 4] and [10, §7.4], where
convergence to the line is conjectured at fixed c; for U (1)c the distance from the line at large n
is conjectured in [12, Conj. 4.2] to be at most of order 2−c , and to vanish only for integers c ≡ 4
modulo 8. What is new here is the heuristic derivation of the offset from the band density.
Degeneracies. Hypothesis (A), sharpened from exponential order to order one, predicts
dn ≃ ρC (tn ) · 12 (tn+1 − tn−1 ) with the full three-Bessel density ρC of section 2.4.
Interior sign change. For t = O(1) the functional is carried by the real sheet through the

Conjecture 1 (conjecture). Let g be the optimal functional and {tn , dn } the extremal spectrum,
in the order of limits of section 2.5.
 (a) ∆best
       1   = c/ΛV + Aopt c1/3 + O(1) with Aopt as in (4.18); there is no log c term at order c1/3
     or above; and (t2 − t1 ) c−1/3 → 0.20705.
 (b) The double zeros of g have density c′ ρU (x)/2 per unit x on the band, and on the lobe interval
     and below the BTZ threshold c1′ log|g| is given by (4.17), up to o(1).
 (c) Deep in the band tn = n + c/24 + O(1) (cf. [11, Figure 4], [10, §7.4]).
 (d) dn ≃ ρC (tn ) · 21 (tn+1 − tn−1 ), with relative error tending to zero away from the tip.
 (e) Between the vacuum and t1 the functional g has exactly one zero, tint → 1/4π, with g > 0
     on (0, tint ) and g < 0 on (tint , t1 ).

The leading term of (a) is Theorem 1; Theorem 5 (hence Corollary 6) proves the upper bound
with 0.483 in place of Aopt ; and the density and the profile of (b) are, in a weak sense, Theorem 7.
Item (a) concerns ∆best
                     1 ; the numerics of section 5 probe the limit P → ∞ of the order-P optimal
gap bounds, which bounds ∆best  1   from above, equality being conjectural (section 2.6).


5     Numerical evidence
[numerical] We compare section 4 with optimal functionals computed numerically: the slope,
then the items of Conjecture 1, then hypothesis (A) itself. No proof in this paper uses the data
of this section.

5.1    Method and convergence
We solve directly for the extremal spectrum {tn , dn } of section 2.2, the dual variables of the
functional problem (the “primal” formulation in the terminology of [11]); by the duality of
                                    (P )
section 2.2 its lowest level is t1 (c). At order P the unknowns are P/2 levels tn and their
weights dn . They are found by Newton’s method [11, 12, 60], continued upward in P from a
linear-programming seed at small P . The solver works in Arb [61] ball arithmetic (interval
arithmetic in midpoint–radius form), used for its precision and not for its error bounds, at 768
bits or more (appendix H.2). Positivity of g above t1 and the pattern of double zeros are checked
afterwards at every (c, P ).
    Coverage. We have full spectra at c = 64, 128, 256, 300, 384, 500, 512, 768, 1024, 1536, 2048,
with P up to 3072. For the gap alone a second, independent implementation of the same method
adds a grid c ∈ {24, . . . , 1800} with P up to 3000; where both ran, they agree to the 20–22 digits
recorded. The solver is calibrated on the exact optima at c = 4 and c = 12 (section 2.2).
                                                                   (P )
    Convergence in the order. Empirically, the order-P bound ∆1 (c) of section 2.2 and its limit
  (∞)
∆1 (c) obey                                           √
                           (P )        (∞)
                         ∆1 (c) − ∆1         (c) ≈ C e−k   P/c
                                                                 ,   k ≈ 4.7–5.7,                    (5.1)
a stretched exponential in P/c. Two things follow. One needs P ≳ c. And an extrapolation by a
                                                   (∞)
pure exponential in P , as in [11], overestimates ∆1 (c), that is, it biases Λ low. We extrapolate
with a stretched exponential whose exponent is free, and quote as uncertainty σ the spread to
cruder extrapolants; the rule is stated in appendix H.2.

against ξ1∗ = 0.0262361 and ΛV = 9.12663. The uncertainty is the spread over fit forms and over

The 9.08 fit. The difference from [11] is in the fitting function, not in the data. We fit the form
used there, c/Λfit + γfit log c + const, not to data but to the exact two-term law c/ΛV + Aopt c1/3 .
On c ∈ [300, 700], the window of [11, §5.2], the fit returns (Λfit , γfit ) = (9.085, 0.48), against the
published (9.08, 0.439). The reason is that c1/3 and log c are nearly collinear over a decade of c;
accordingly the result drifts with the window, from (9.02, 0.28) on [20, 300] to about (9.11, 0.71)
on [500, 4000]. The two other fitting functions of [11], c/A + B1 and c/A + B1 + B2 /c, treated in
the same way return A = 9.004 and 9.043, against their 9.01 and 9.04. The published spread is
thus reproduced, to within 0.01 in each entry, by a law of slope 9.127: the earlier extrapolation
is explained, not contradicted.

5.3    Fine structure
We check the items of Conjecture 1 in turn, and then (A). Item (a): the fitted coefficient of
c−2/3 in t1 /c lies between 0.269 and 0.285, depending on the form and the window, against
Aopt = 0.27666; and a term γ log c allowed to compete with c1/3 comes out with γ between −0.07
and −0.005, compatible with zero. The first spacing (t2 − t1 ) c−1/3 decreases from 0.28963 at
c = 64 to 0.22369 at c = 768 and extrapolates, in the basis {1, c−2/3 , c−1 }, to 0.20700, against
0.20705 (two other correction forms give 0.2043 and 0.2085). Item (b): the spacings sn of the
double zeros, with sn − 1 extrapolated linearly in 1/c, agree with 1/ρV − 1 to within 0.1% for
                                                                                   1
0.06 ≤ ξ ≤ 0.20. Item (c): deep in the band    
                                                 the coefficient of c in tn − n is 24 (1 ± 4 · 10−4 ). Item
                               1
(d): the ratio dn / ρC (tn ) · 2 (tn+1 − tn−1 ) is 1 − 10 deep in the band and 0.98 at n = 2; this
                                                         −6

is the forced part of (A), (4.6), verified to far better than exponential order. Item (e): at every
(c, P ) there is exactly one zero between the vacuum and t1 , with the predicted signs; it moves
from tint = 0.0803889 at c = 64 to 0.0796944 at c = 500, that is, tint = 1/4π + 0.058/c + o(1/c).
The Cardy height. Hypothesis (A) itself is tested through its consequence (4.8), that is,
through the Cardy height κ of section 4.2. It can be read off the non-real zeros of the truncated
polynomials polP . At every (c, P ) these lie on one parabola in the complex t-plane, with
focuspat the BTZ threshold t = 0, that is, on the lines |Im b| = κ2 + O(1/c) in the variable
b = 3t/c′ . A fit to the vertex of the parabola for 64 ≤ c ≤ 500, which does not involve ξ1∗ ,
gives κ = 1.0000 ± 0.0005; for comparison, κ = 1.0107 would correspondR
                                                                               to the slope 9.080. The
reason the zeros sit there is that the duality integral ĝ(s) = 2π g J0 of section 2.3, continued
to complex s = c′ b2 /3, converges absolutely exactly for |Im b| < 12 : the Bessel kernel grows like
 4π ′                                            ′         2π ′
e 3 c x|Im b| , and on the band g decays like e−c L(x) = e− 3 c x . At finite P the zeros condense on
the boundary of this region. This is the strip of section 6.3, seen in the data.


Part III         Optimality
Everything in this Part is proved, and no computer is used. It needs only section 2. The strip of
section 4.2 and the curve of section 4.4 reappear here as lemmas about a domain and a potential,
without hypotheses; a reader who skipped Part II loses nothing.


6     Optimality: nothing beyond ΛV
We show that the spinless bootstrap cannot give a slope better than ΛV , at any order and with
any functional in Ā. The mechanism is the tension described after (2.16). A functional that
certifies the gap bound t1 must carry half of its Cardy-weighted mass below t1 ; and we prove that
no function in Ā, feasible or not, carries more than an exponentially small fraction of its mass
below t = ξ1∗ c′ . The statements are in section 6.1. The proof is an electrostatics problem on the
strip of figure 2, described in section 6.2. Section 6.3 continues the functional to the strip and
bounds it on the boundary, section 6.4 collects the potential theory that we use, section 6.5 turns
the bounds on the boundary into a bound in the interior by a harmonic function, section 6.6
finds that function in closed form, and sections 6.7 and 6.8 draw the consequences. The technical
lemmas are in appendices B and C.

6.1    Statements
Theorem 3 (Concentration; proved). Let x∗1 = arcsinh(1)/π. For every δ ∈ (0, x∗1 ) there is a
constant Cδ < ∞ such that for all c ≥ 40 and every g ∈ Ā, with no condition on the sign of g,
                             Z c′ (x∗ −δ)2 /3
                                    1
                                                |g| V dt ≤ Cδ c′ 4 e−κ(δ) c M [g].
                                                                          ′
                                                                                                     (6.1)
                              0


                                                         26
Here κ(δ) > 0 is the explicit Cardy deficit, given in closed form in Lemma 6.7. It satisfies
                          q √
                                                  
              κ(δ) = 98    2 2 π δ 3/2 1 + O(δ) ,          κ(δ) ≥ 2.228 δ 3/2   (0 < δ ≤ x∗1 ),          (6.2)
           q √
where 98    2 2 π = 2.650, and Cδ ≤ Cconc δ −5 with an absolute constant Cconc .
In words: weighted by the density of states that the vacuum demands, any bootstrap function
has exponentially little mass below t ≈ 0.0262 c′ .
Theorem 4 (Optimality; proved). Let ξ1∗ and ΛV be as in Theorem 1, and call g weakly feasible
if it is weakly feasible at some T , hence at T = t1 (g). For every Λ > ΛV there is c0 (Λ) such that
for c ≥ c0 (Λ) no weakly feasible g ∈ Ā, g ̸≡ 0, has t1 (g) + a ≤ c/Λ. Quantitatively, there is a
central charge copt
                  0 , explicit in principle and not computed, such that

Proof of the first statement of Theorem 4 from Theorem 3. Let Λ > ΛV , that is, Λ1 − 12              1
                                                                                                        < ξ1∗ ,
and choose δ ∈ (0, x∗1 ) with 3 (x∗1 − δ)2 > Λ − 12 . If t1 (g) + a ≤ c/Λ, then t1 (g) ≤ c′ ( Λ − 12 ) + Λ1 ≤
                                1             1   1                                           1   1

c′ (x∗1 − δ)2 /3 as soon as c is large. If moreover g is weakly feasible, g−R is supported on [0, t1 ] and
the half-mass condition (2.16) makes the left side of (6.1) at least 0t1 g− V dt ≥ 21 M [g], where
M [g] > 0 because g ̸≡ 0. Hence 12 ≤ Cδ c′4 e−κ(δ)c , which is false for large c. How large c must
                                                        ′


be depends on Λ only.

The rate (6.3) is proved in section 6.8. Three remarks. First, the theorem quantifies over all of Ā,
and c0 (Λ) does not depend on g. Since Ā contains the functionals of every order P (Lemma 2.6),
the statement is uniform in P : for c ≥ c0 (Λ) no amount of computing produces a spinless bound
below c/Λ. Second, this is a statement about the method, the linear program of section 2.2, and
not about conformal field theories. Third, the informative content of the quantitative form is its
rate; the right side of (6.3) lies below the trivial floor t1 ≥ 0 of section 2.4 for c′ ≲ 414.

Attribution. The strategy is the following: continue the functional to a strip; control one part
of the boundary by the total mass and the other by the eigen-identity; bound the function inside
by its harmonic majorant, the harmonic function with the same boundary data, which lies above
it by the maximum principle; and conclude through the half mass of the negative part. This is
the strategy of the proof for the Cohn–Elkies problem at large dimension in [13, Ch. 1, Prop. 3.1
and Thm. 3.8]; we have read the statements and the structure of that argument but have not
independently verified it; nothing in this paper depends on it logically. There d → ∞ and the
strip is in the Mellin-transform variable, of half-width d/2; here d = 2 is fixed and the strip is
in the rescaled dimension b itself, its width being fixed by the requirement that the continued
kernel have exactly Cardy growth on the edge. The strip through the vacuum, the weight V ,
the slit geometry and the closed form are specific to Virasoro. The argument in position space
does not run for U (1)c . The argument in the Mellin variable does run for Virasoro: the strip is
then replaced by a domain with a free boundary (appendix A). The last step of the strategy, the
half mass of the negative part, goes back to Bourgain, Clozel and Kahane [52] (see also [53]);
Cohn and Gonçalves [51, §3.1] use it for the functions with fˆ = −f of the twin problem, and
(2.16) is the same step with the Cardy weight. A mass-concentration principle similar to that
of [13, Ch. 1] has since been proved for Delsarte’s linear program for binary codes [62].

6.2    Idea of the proof: an electrostatics problem
The stage is figure 2(b). In the variable b of (2.17) a functional g ∈ Ā becomes the function
G(b) = g(c′ b2 /3), and the proof has five steps.

     in which a potential is −∞ at its positive charges (section 6.4). It is subharmonic, that is,
     it obeys the maximum principle. We cut the strip along |x| ≥ s, so that this part of the
     real axis becomes boundary, where step (3) applies, and the interval (−s, s), about which
     nothing is assumed, stays inside. The value of a harmonic function inside is an average of
     its boundary values, so a bound in the mean suffices: ug lies below the harmonic function
     that is zero on the edges and −L on the slits, up to o(1) (Proposition 6.4).
 (5) For s = x∗1 that harmonic function is explicit, and it lies strictly below the Cardy line on
     |x| < x∗1 (Lemmas 6.5 and 6.7). Since |g| V ≈ ec (ug +L) M [g], almost no Cardy-weighted
                                                            ′


     mass can sit there.
Table 3 is the dictionary. In its language the half-mass condition says that the potential of a
weakly feasible functional must come within o(1) of the Cardy line somewhere on |x| ≤ x1 (g),
and the theorem says that the first point where this is possible is x∗1 .

On the slits G(x) = g(c′ x2 /3) is the functional in the region where a good functional is non-
negative. Nothing is known there pointwise, and an extremal functional vanishes there infinitely
often; (6.6) is a statement in the mean, and section 6.5 uses it as such.

Remark (Why c′ = c − 1 and not c; orientation). The scaling t = c′ b2 /3 puts the edge of the
strip through the true vacuum t = −a, where the kernel is bounded by Ka , which exceeds V
by a power of c′ only. With c in place of c′ the edge would pass through t = −c/12, √ where the
                                                                               (π/3) 3t/c
kernel is bounded only by Ka+1/12 , which exceeds V by a factor growing like e            . Since
feasible functionals of high order carry Cardy-weighted mass arbitrarily far out in t, the edge
bound would then depend on the functional, and the uniformity in g would be lost.

What is not used: sign information (positivity enters only through the half-mass condition, in
one line, at the very end); information about g near the BTZ threshold beyond integrability;
any property of optimal functionals. The inputs on Bessel functions are Lemmas B.1 to B.3.

6.4    Toolkit: potential theory in physicist’s terms
Steps (4) and (5) of section 6.2 are two-dimensional electrostatics. The box collects the notions
and the four formulas that we use, first in the language of charges and potentials and then
precisely. We use the sign convention in which a unit point charge at ζ has the potential log|b − ζ|.


                                                           29
Toolkit: potential theory
Subharmonic functions. If G ̸≡ 0 is holomorphic, then
                                                X
                         ∇2 log|G(b)| = 2π                 mζ δ 2 (b − ζ) ≥ 0,                 (6.7)
                                              ζ: G(ζ)=0

with mζ the multiplicity: log|G| is the potential of positive point charges at the zeros of G,
and it is harmonic away from them. A function u with ∇2 u ≥ 0 in the sense of distributions
is called subharmonic; precisely, u is upper semicontinuous with values in [−∞, ∞) (it may
jump down, as at a charge, but not up), and u(b) is at most the mean of u over every small
circle around b. Such a function obeys the maximum principle: if h is harmonic on a bounded
domain and lim sup u ≤ h at every boundary point, then u ≤ h inside [63, Thm. 2.3.1].
Adding positive charges lowers the potential.
Harmonic measure. For a domain Ω and a point b ∈ Ω, ωb (E) is the probability that a
Brownian path started at b first leaves Ω through the part E of the boundary. Equivalently,
bR 7→ ωb (E) solves the Dirichlet problem with boundary values 1 on E and 0 elsewhere, and
    φ dωb , the Poisson integral of φ, solves it with boundary values φ [63, §4.3]; for the upper
 half-plane dωz = Im z dσ/(π|σ − z|2 ), the Poisson kernel. On the slit strip the two lips of a
 slit are different boundary points, and dωb /dx denotes the density of ωb on a slit with the
 two lips added. It is explicit through the conformal map of Lemma 6.3. Like the charge
 density at the rim of a thin conducting plate, it has an inverse square-root singularity at the
 tips.
Harmonic majorant. This is the solution h of the Dirichlet problem whose data are the
 boundary values of a subharmonic u, that is, the potential with the same Rboundary values
 and with all interior charges removed. By the maximum principle u ≤ h = u dωb [63, §4.1].
Poisson–Jensen. For G bounded and holomorphic on Ω, with boundary values taken
 ωb -almost everywhere,                          Z
                                  log|G(b)| ≤          log|G| dωb .                            (6.8)
                                                  ∂Ω
                                                                                         P
If G is holomorphic on a neighborhood of Ω̄, the right side minus the left side is ζ mζ gΩ (b, ζ),
where gΩ ≥ 0 is the Green’s function of Ω and the sum runs over the zeros of G in Ω [63, §4.5]:
each zero lowers the potential by the potential of a unit charge in the grounded domain. We
need (6.8) for a merely bounded function that may vanish on the boundary (Lemma C.3(a));
for the disk this is classical: removing the charges at the zeros, inside or on the boundary,
only raises the potential [64, Ch. II, Thm. 4.1 and Cor. 4.2], [65, Thms. 17.16–17.17].
Jensen’s inequality with entropy. On the slits we know only a weighted mean of |G|
(Lemma 6.2), whereas (6.8) asks for the mean of log|G| with respect to ωb . Let w be a
probability density on a set S of the real line and K > 0 a weight. Since the mean of a
logarithm is at most the logarithm of the mean [65, Thm. 3.3],
             Z                        Z                Z                     Z

                         ′

dωb /dx the entropy is finite despite the singularity at the tips; it is bounded in Lemma C.2,
together with its dependence on the distance from b to the tips.
Phragmén–Lindelöf. On an unbounded domain the maximum principle needs a growth
condition: on the strip S the function cosh(πx) cos(πy) is harmonic and vanishes on both
edges. A harmonic function on S that vanishes on the edges and is O(eα|x| ) for some α < π
is zero [63, §2.3]. All our functions grow at most linearly in |x|. The form that we use is
proved in the proof of Lemma 6.5.

6.5   The slit strip and the majorant
We now choose the domain on which the maximum principle is applied. For s > 0 let

                       Ds := S \ {x + iy : |x| ≥ s, y = 0},           D := Dx∗1 .                      (6.11)

Ds is simply connected. Its boundary consists of the two edges and of two slits, and each slit
has two lips, which count as different boundary points; the ends of the slits, b = ±s, are the tips.
We prove everything for D; the choice of s is discussed in section 6.6. The next lemma makes
harmonic measure on D explicit.

Lemma 6.3 (Conformal map; proved). The function

correspond to the four ends of D (figure 4(b), last row of labels).

The proof is Lemma C.1(a),(b); part (c) of that lemma transports the Poisson kernel of H to
D and gives the density of ωb , its exponential decay along the slits and its first moment. The
map is the uniformization of section 4.4, (4.11); it is now a lemma about a domain and not a
hypothesis about a curve.

ug ≤ −L(x) + o(1) in the mean with respect to ωb . The entropy is the one place where the
geometry of D, namely the square-root singularity of harmonic measure at the tips, has to be
examined by hand. Poisson–Jensen (6.8) then bounds ug (b) by the mean of these data, and the
mean of the data is a harmonic function of b.

linearly decreasing potential −L. The function Us no longer knows anything about g or about
                                c′ U ∗ (b)
conformal field theory, and e x1 is, up to a power of c′ , a universal bound for |G|/M [g] on
compact subsets of D, in particular below the BTZ threshold and on the lobe interval. Nothing
is imposed on the interval (−s, s). That is where the mass of a would-be better functional has
to be, by the half-mass condition, and the point of the construction is that ug is small there all
the same.

6.6    Why s = x∗1 ; the extremal potential in closed form; the zero law
[orientation] Why this slit. For any s the argument of section 6.5 gives the majorant Us on Ds ,
and it bounds the Cardy-weighted mass wherever Us + L < 0 inside (−s, s). A harmonic    p     function
on a slit domain generically has a square-root singularity at a tip: Us + L ≃ γtip (s) s − |x| inside
the tips. The coefficient γtip changes sign exactly once, at s = x∗1 ; numerically γtip = −0.16,
−0.02, ≈ 0, +0.02, +0.16 at s = 0.24, 0.275, x∗1 , 0.285, 0.32. For s < x∗1 one finds Us + L < 0 on
                                                                       1    2
all of (−s, s), and the method gives the weaker bound Λ ≤ 1/( 12         + s3 ); for instance s = 0.26
gives Λ ≤ 9.45. For s > x∗1 the majorant rises above the Cardy line inside the tips and excludes
nothing there. Thus s = x∗1 is the largest useful slit, characterized by tangency: Us + L vanishes
to order 32 at the tips, which is a free-boundary condition. None of this is used in the proofs.
    For s = x∗1 the Dirichlet problem (6.14) has a solution in closed form. The idea is to guess

constant on the edges, Im p must be ∓ 12 on the slits and 0 on the edges.

and p is related to b by sinh πb = i sinh πp, which is the curve (4.13): the spectral curve of
section 4 is the complex electric field of the extremal potential.

Proof. We compute the boundary values of p, integrate them, and prove uniqueness by a barrier
argument; one identity between two integrals makes the constant√on the edges vanish.
    (a) The function p. The principal branch arcsinh w = log(w + 1 + w2 ) is the inverse of sinh
on the strip |Im ζ| < π2 ; it is holomorphic on C \ ±i[1, ∞). For b = x + iy ∈ S the point −i sinh πb
lies on a cut if and only if sinh πb = sinh πx cos πy + i cosh πx sin πy is real and of modulus at
least one. Since |y| < 12 this means y = 0 and |sinh πx| ≥ 1, that is, b real with |b| ≥ x∗1 . So p is
holomorphic on D, with p(0) = 0, p(−b) = −p(b) and p(b̄) = −p(b). Furthermore |Im p| < 12 ,
and sinh πp = −i sinh πb gives sinh2 (π Re p) ≤ |sinh πp|2 = sinh2 πx + sin2 πy ≤ cosh2 πx, hence
|Re p(b)| ≤ |Re b| + 1. The values on the axes and on the boundary are
                  p(x) = − πi arcsin(sinh πx)         (|x| < x∗1 ),        p(iy) = π1 arcsinh(sin πy),                    p(± 2i ) = ±x∗1 ,
 p(x ± 2i ) = ± π1 arcsinh(cosh πx),                                  p(x ± i0) = ± π1 arccosh(sinh πx) − 2i                           (x > x∗1 ).
The first three hold because −i sinh πb equals −i sinh πx, sin πy and ± cosh πx at these points.
For the last, −i sinh π(x ± iϵ) tends to the point −i sinh πx of the lower cut from the right and
from the left, and arcsinh(±η − iσ) → ± arccosh σ − iπ     2 as η ↓ 0, for σ > 1. On the left slit the
values follow from p(−b) = −p(b). In particular Im p = 0 on E± , Im p = − 12 on both lips of the
right slit and Im p = + 12 on both lips of the left slit; Re p(x + i0) = π1 arccosh(sinh π|x|) on the
upper lips of both slits; and p extends continuously toR the edges, the lips and the tips.

on E± . This is the place where the number arcsinh(1)/π enters the rigorous argument.
    (d) Uniqueness. Let d be harmonic on D and continuous up to the edges, the lips and the
tips, with boundary value 0 there, and let |d(b)| ≤ Cd (1 + |Re b|). We claim that d ≡ 0. For ϵ > 0
let dϵ (b) := d(b) − ϵ cosh(2 Re b) cos(2 Im b), which is harmonic on D. The subtracted term (the
barrier) is positive on S̄, where cos(2 Im b) ≥ cos 1 > 0, so dϵ ≤ 0 at the edges, the lips and the
tips. On the cross-cuts {|Re b| = X} ∩ D we have dϵ ≤ Cd (1 + X) − ϵ cos 1 cosh 2X < 0 as soon
as X ≥ X(ϵ). The maximum principle on the bounded domains D ∩ {|Re b| < X} gives dϵ ≤ 0
on D. Letting ϵ → 0 we get d ≤ 0, and the same argument for −d gives d ≡ 0. Applied to the
difference of two solutions, this shows that (6.14) with sR= x∗1 has at most one solution. By (b)
and (c), U is a solution, and so is the Poisson integral − L dωb over the slits, by Lemma C.1(c)
(appendix C.4). Hence both are equal to Ux∗1 , which is (C.15).

6.7        The Cardy deficit and the proof of Theorem 3
On the lobe interval the extremal potential lies strictly below the Cardy line, and the distance
between the two is what Theorem 3 exponentiates.

Of the four powers of c′ , the edges and the slits cost at most two between them (Lemma 6.1,
Lemma B.2(e); their weights in the Poisson–Jensen mean add up to one), stated as three
in (C.16), and t2δ /a ≤ Cc′ gives one more. The window of width O(1/c′ ) at the BTZ threshold
enters only through Lemma B.3, in the edge bound, because Lemma B.2(d) holds down to t = 0.

What this says about section 4. The composition of p with the map of Lemma 6.3 is the
function p(z) of (4.11), and sinh πb = i sinh πp. The curve has thus been recovered without
hypotheses, as the solution of the linear boundary-value problem (6.14). In section 4 it rests on
a selection rule and describes the best functional, conjecturally; here it bounds every functional.
[numerical] The profiles ug of the numerical optima of section 5 exceed U nowhere by more
than O(1/c′ ), and at the threshold they approach it from below: at (c, P ) = (64, 256), (128, 2048),
(256, 2048), (512, 2688) the difference U − ug at b = 0 is 0.053, 0.035, 0.022, 0.013 (figure 6).
The majorant is approached at leading order by the numerical optima. Whether it is provably
attained is the subject of Part IV.

                                                           35
6.8     The effective rate
We prove (6.3). The idea is to let δ tend to zero with c in Theorem 3, as slowly as the
prefactor allows. For this one needs the dependence of Cδ on δ. Its only source is the entropy of
harmonic measure seen from a real point at distance δ from a tip, which is at most CH + 5 log 1δ
by Lemma C.2(ii); exponentiated, this is Cδ ≤ Cconc δ −5 , as the proof of Theorem 3 shows.
Everything else is uniform in δ.

Proof of the√second statement of Theorem 4. Let g ∈ Ā, g ̸≡ 0, be weakly feasible and t1 = t1 (g).
