Part IV            Attainment
Section 7 is proved with computer assistance, and sections 8 and 10 are proved. Sections 7 and 8
use section 2 and, from section 6, in the proofs only Lemma 6.5 and Definition 6.6, the closed
form of U and the zero law; section 10 uses in addition Proposition 6.4 and Lemma 6.7. Section 7
builds a feasible g ∈ Ā at ξ1∗ c′ + O(c′1/3 ); section 8 shows that any feasible g ∈ Ā bounds every
CFT. Section 9 describes a second functional that attains the slope, at a physicist’s level of
rigor; nothing else depends on it. Section 10 shows that a functional that comes close to the
bound has no choice: its zeros follow the zero law on which the functional of section 7 is built.


7      Attainment: an explicit functional reaching ΛV
We construct, for every large central charge, one explicit function g ∈ Ā that is feasible at
ξ1∗ c′ + O(c′1/3 ). With Theorem 4 this proves the slope, Theorem 1. The function is an infinite
product of elementary factors, and its zeros are placed according to the zero law of Definition 6.6.
The construction is short (sections 7.1 and 7.2). The work is to prove that the product and its
Fourier transform combine to a function that is positive on the band and has a positive vacuum
value. Section 7.3 explains how this is done, without proofs. The proofs are in sections 7.4
to 7.10, and the long ones in appendices D to G, which may be skipped at first. The inequalities
verified by computer are marked ⟨cert⟩ and listed in table 4 of appendix H.



                                                     36
7.1    Image charges on the strip, and the zero law as a blueprint
The idea is electrostatic. A zero of a holomorphic function is a unit charge for the logarithm
of its modulus, and by Proposition 6.4 the profile ug of every functional, (6.13), lies below the
potential U of a definite charge distribution on the strip S, up to O(log c′ /c′ ). To certify the gap
bound x∗1 , the profile must touch the Cardy line −L where U does. This suggests building a
functional whose profile is U : charges on the slits at density ρU , and on the edges a modulus no
larger than Lemma 6.1 allows. (Theorem 7 shows that the profile of every attaining sequence is
U .) So we want zeros at density c′ ρU on the band and modulus one on the edges, and we build
them out of the potential of one point charge between two grounded plates.

Glossary. A site χn is a point of the real b-axis at which a zero, or a conjugate pair of zeros,
is placed, and cell n is the interval (χn , χn+1 ). The comb is the product B of image-charge
factors over the sites. Detuning means moving a real double zero to a conjugate pair χn ± iεn .
The onset is the neighborhood of the first kept site χ2 ; the comb omits the first site of the law
(section 7.2). The Airy length ℓc = (κtip c′ )−2/3 is the scale of the spacing of the first sites, and
the hand-off point xI = x∗1 + c′−θon , with θon = 0.2, is where the onset (Region I) ends and
steepest descent takes over (Region II). Four functions that are defined in sections 7.5 and 7.6
appear already in section 7.3: the site index ν(x), which equals k − 2 at the site χk , with the
comb phase Ψ = 2πν − π; the envelope Env of the undetuned comb; the level Lev, the explicit
unit of all ratios; and the amplitude Amp of the transform.

  Toolkit: image charges on the strip

  One factor. For ζ with |Im ζ| < 12 let

                                    π(b − ζ)      π(b + ζ)   cosh πb − cosh πζ
                   Tζ (b) := tanh            tanh          =                   .               (7.1)
                                       2             2       cosh πb + cosh πζ
  For real ζ, − log|Tζ (b)| is the Green’s function of the strip with poles at ±ζ: the potential
  at b of unit charges at ±ζ between the two grounded plates E± . The zeros and poles of
  Tζ are the successive mirror images of ±ζ in the plates (figure 7a). The map w = tanh πb     2
  takes S onto the unit disk and turns Tζ into a product of two Blaschke factors [64]; Tζ is the
  Blaschke factor of the strip. We use four facts.
  (T1) Modulus. For real ζ, |Tζ | = 1 on both edges and |Tζ | < 1 in S. The same holds for
  the product Tζ Tζ̄ of a conjugate pair, ζ = χ + iε with 0 < ε < 12 . A bounded holomorphic
  function on S whose modulus is one on the edges is called inner; a zero-free factor such as
  sech2 πb
        2 , which is determined by its modulus on the edges, is outer.
  (T2) Pairs on the real axis. For real χ, 0 ≤ ε < 12 and real x,
                                            Y cosh π(x ∓ χ) − cos πε
                    Tχ+iε (x) Tχ−iε (x) =                              ≥ Tχ (x)2 ,
                                            ±
                                              cosh π(x ∓ χ) + cos πε

  and the left side is positive if ε > 0: lifting a double zero off the axis raises the modulus on
  the axis.
  (T3) Reflection. Tζ (b + i) = 1/Tζ (b), and Tζ ( 2i ) = −1 for every ζ.
  (T4) Zeros and poles. For Re ζ ̸= 0, Tζ has simple zeros at {±ζ} + 2iZ, simple poles at
  {±ζ} + i(2Z + 1), and no others.
  All four follow from (7.1). For (T1) note that cosh πb is imaginary on E± , so that numerator
  and denominator have the same modulus when cosh πζ is real, and numerator and denomi-
  nator of the pair are conjugate to each other in general; the maximum principle gives the
  inequality inside.

   The next lemma says that the zero law is the right density for these factors. It is the
continuum limit of every sum over sites in this section.
Lemma 7.1 (Riesz representation of U ; proved). For every b in the closed strip S̄,
                                                 Z ∞

and the integral converges absolutely. In particular the left side vanishes on E± and equals −L(x)
at real b = x with |x| ≥ x∗1 .
The proof is in appendix D.1: both sides have the same charge on S, both vanish on the edges,
and both grow at most linearly; a potential with these three properties is unique. In words: a
                                                                                 ′
continuum of factors Tx′ at the density of the law reproduces the Cardy decay e−c L on the band
exactly, and is invisible in modulus on the edges.

Notation for the edge. On the edge E+ we write b = v + 2i with v real. The function p of
Lemma 6.5 is real there, and we call

the edge function. It is even, convex, and increasing on [0, ∞), with F (0) = x∗1 and 0 < F ′ < 1

7.2        The functional and the theorem
The functional is the zero law made discrete. The sites are the quantiles of the counting function
N : the points χn > x∗1 with c′ N (χn ) = n − 14 , for n = 2, 3, . . . ; the site n = 1 of the law is

the product integrable, and antisymmetrize under the Fourier transform:

In words: a product of image-charge factors at the quantiles of the zero law, minus its own
Fourier transform, is a functional that certifies a gap within O(c1/3 ) of c/ΛV . The offset +1 is
cosmetic: the proof gives g > 0 already from t⋆ + o(1/c′ ) on (section 7.8). The next proposition
collects what is elementary about h0 .

Proposition 7.2 (Elementary properties; proved). Let c ≥ 40, the standing assumption of
section 2.6. Then maxn εn < 12 , the product B converges locally uniformly on |Im b| < 1−maxn εn ,
and:
 (a) |B| ≤ 1 on S̄ and |B| = 1 on E± .

     h0 (t0 ) − 2h0 (t0 + 1) + h0 (t0 + 2) satisfies |h0,vac | ≤ 8.
 (c) On the physical axis, h0 < 0 on the lobe interval [0, t⋆ ), h0 has a simple zero at t⋆ , and
     h0 > 0 for t > t⋆ .
 (d) On the edge, |h0 (v + 2i )| = 2/ cosh πv exactly.
On the edge through the vacuum the comb is invisible in modulus, and all the information that
it carries is a phase.

Proof. First, maxn εn < 21 ; this places every pair under (T1) and keeps the poles of B out

                                                          2

    The threshold catt
                     0 is named before it is fixed. Throughout this section and appendices D
to G, a statement made for c ≥ catt      0 , or for c large, holds for c above a threshold of its own,
which may depend on a parameter that is named there (r in Lemma 7.5, ϵ in Proposition 7.4).
The proof of Theorem 5 meets finitely many of these thresholds, each at a fixed value of its
parameter; none of them depends on a later one, and catt       0 is the largest of them. It is fixed at
the end of that proof, in section 7.10.

7.3    Idea of the proof
There are three things to prove: (i) g > 0 on the band t ≥ t⋆ + 1; (ii) gvac > 0; (iii) g ∈ Ā.
The relation ĝ = −g is automatic. Since h0 ≥ 0 on the band, everything in (i) reduces to one
statement: ĥ0 is smaller than h0 there. Figure 8 shows this at c = 256. The function h0 is
a train of positive bumps, one per cell. Its transform oscillates in lock-step with it, with an
amplitude of about 12 in units of the level Lev; this amplitude is about 0.29 of the height of the
bumps. The difference 2g = h0 − ĥ0 stays positive.

How ĥ0 is computed. The transform at a band point x is an integral of h0 times a Hankel
kernel along the edge E+ of the strip (section 7.4; figure 2). On the edge |h0 | is explicit and
                                                                                    ′
does not depend on the comb (Proposition 7.2(d)), and the kernel has modulus e−c L(x) . So the
size of the integrand is pinned at the Cardy level, and the comb enters through a phase only.
The phase has two stationary points ±v0 (x) on the edge, and they exist exactly when x ≥ x∗1 :
this is how the band appears in the transform.

Three regimes. Three scales organize the proof, ℓc ≪ c′−θon ≪ c′−1/(4n1 ) ; they are drawn
at the bottom of figure 8. In Region II, x ≥ xI (sections 7.6 and 7.7), steepest descent gives
|ĥ0 | ≤ Amp (1 + o(1)) with an explicit amplitude Amp ≤ 0.504 Lev, and the detuning gives a
floor h0 ≥ µ Lev with µ > 0.504. Positivity is one inequality between explicit functions that
does not involve c, and no information on the phase of ĥ0 is used. In Region I, χ2 ≤ x ≤ xI
(section 7.8), the two saddles
                           √ coalesce     and the asymptotics is not uniform. There we use only
                                  ′L
the crude bound |ĥ0 | ≤ C c e
                             ′ −c    , and the fact that near the onset the comb stands above the
Cardy level by a power of c′ . We call this the overshoot. It is there because the charge of the
omitted site is missing from the comb. The two regions overlap, because the overshoot beats the

crude bound up to x − x∗1 ≈ c′−1/(4n1 ) , which is beyond xI . For the vacuum (section 7.9), gvac is
the mass of the lobe minus the mass of the band, up to O(1). Both are dominated by a window
of width ℓc around the onset, and the band is at most a fixed fraction Rvac < 1 of the lobe
(Proposition 7.13). The computer enters as follows. Once the factors that tend to one as c → ∞
are bounded by hand, each regime leaves inequalities that do not involve c, for explicit functions
of one real variable: for the amplitude and the floor in Region II, for finite products over the
first cells in Region I, and for a ratio of two integrals at the vacuum. These inequalities, marked
⟨cert⟩, are proved in interval arithmetic (appendix H.1); nothing that depends on c is computed.

Why each ingredient is there. Sites at the quantiles of the law. Besides the Cardy decay
            ′
|B| ≈ e−c L on the band (Lemma 7.1), this makes the phase of the integrand on the edge
stationary exactly at the points ±v0 (x) that correspond to band points x, where the phase of B
advances by 2π per site (D.13).
    Detuning by η0 = 14 . The undetuned comb is |h00 | = Env sin2 πν. It returns to zero in every
cell, while its transform does not, so g would be negative near every site. Lifting the zeros off
the axis creates a floor above 12 Lev. It changes nothing on the edge, where a conjugate pair
is still unimodular, so the amplitude of the transform is untouched. The value matters: for
η0 = 0.2 the floor of Lemma 7.9 is below 0.45, which is not enough.
    Why the amplitude is 12 . Write h00 = 12 Env (1 + cos Ψ). The transform reproduces the
oscillating part 12 Env cos Ψ. It cannot see the mean, which has no stationary phase.
    The omitted site, m = 1. It makes the first zero simple. It produces the overshoot at the
onset. And it makes the vacuum lopsided in favor of the lobe. It costs A1 c1/3 in the bound,
and nothing at the order of the slope. With no site omitted and the same node phase one finds


                                                                                                           41
numerically |Lobe|/Band ≈ 0.8 [numerical], and that comb is not feasible.
    The node phase 14 . This is a convention and is not tuned. Placing nodes at the quantiles
of an equilibrium density is a Bohr–Sommerfeld rule, familiar from the choice of interpolation
nodes in the numerical bootstrap [66, Sec. 3.3]. Only n1 = 74 enters the certified constants. With
1
4 the phase of the transform coincides with that of the comb deep in the band (figure 8c).
    The outer factor sech2 and the simple zero at χ2 . The outer factor gives integrability and
membership in Ā, and it cancels from the ratio Amp/Lev up to the factor coth πx    2 . The zero is
simple because a feasible g must change sign once, at t1 .

7.5    The comb on the real axis: site index, envelope, level
On the band, log|B(x)| is a sum over the sites of the potentials log|Tχk (x)|. By Lemma 7.1 the
integral which this sum approximates is −c′ L(x), corrected for the omitted charge. So h0 is the
Cardy decay, times the effect of the omitted charge, times a bounded function of the position of
x in its cell. This subsection names these pieces and states two-sided bounds for them. The
proofs are in appendix F. They use nothing about ĥ0 .

Site index and envelope. For real a > 14 (as the index of a site, here and in appendices F
and G, the letter a is a real number and not c′ /12) let χa be the point with c′ N (χa ) = a − 14 ;
the sites are the integer values of a. Let ℓn := χn+1 − χn be the length of cell n, and for x in cell
n let u := (x − χn )/ℓn ∈ (0, 1) be the position in the cell. The site index and the comb phase are

               ν(x) := c′ N (x) + 14 − 2,              ν(χa ) = a − 2,        Ψ(x) := 2πν(x) − π,             (7.10)

so that ν runs over (n − 2, n − 1) on cell n. Beyond χ2 the undetuned comb has its double zeros
exactly where sin2 πν has them. The envelope is therefore defined, for x > χ2 , by
                                                                                            
                      |h00 (x)| = Env(x) sin2 πν(x) = 21 Env(x) 1 + cos Ψ(x) ,                                (7.11)

and is positive and smooth there. Off the sites, h0 = h00 Π with Π := |B|/|B 0 | ≥ 1 by (T2).

Hole functions. A hole function is the potential at x of the sites nearest to x, kept exactly,
minus that of the piece of continuum of the same charge which they replace. All other sites are
replaced by the continuum, by an inequality with a definite sign and no remainder: apart from
the sites far to the left of x, the summand a 7→ log|Tχa (x)| is concave on either side of x, and
for a concave function the trapezoid rule lies below the integral and the midpoint rule above it
(appendix F.2). For x in cell n and j ≥ 1, the upper bound (for B 0 ) keeps the 2j + 2 nearest

functions h±j of the position u in the cell alone, (F.41) and (F.42). For j = 1 we write h∞ := h1 :

7.6    The transform by steepest descent
We compute ĥ0 on the band by stationary phase in the edge representation (7.8). This needs
the phase of B on the edge (Lemma 7.6) and a saddle-point lemma with explicit remainders
(Lemma 7.7). The comb drops out of the amplitude Amp, which depends only on the outer
factor and on F .

7.9    The vacuum inequality
The vacuum value is a competition between the negative lobe of h0 below t⋆ and the positive
band beyond it, both weighted with the Cardy weight V . The lobe wins: the proof gives the
factor 1/Rvac ≥ 8. The omitted site is what makes it so: the stretch from the first site of the
law to χ2 , which would otherwise be the first cell of the band, belongs to the lobe.
    By Lemma 7.3(b) and Proposition 7.2(b),(c),

Membership. On the real axis |h0 | ≤ 4e−πx |B|. By Lemma 7.5(c), |B| ≤ C(c) e−c L for

                                                                                        ′
and the kernel of (2.9) is real. For x ≥ xI the bound (7.25) gives |ĥ0 | ≤ C(c) e−c L−πx : indeed

we have |ĥ0 | ≤ Cc′ by Lemma 7.10, and e−c L−πx is bounded below. This is the hypothesis

real and continuous on [0, ∞). The three conditions of Definition 2.5 follow. Condition (i) is
the integrability just shown. Condition (ii) is Lemma 7.3(a). For (iii), V ≤ (4π 2 t/a) ec L by

Lemma B.2(d), so |g| V ≤ C(c) t e−πx , which is integrable.

gives g > 0 for x ≥ xI , and section 7.8 gives g > 0 for t⋆ + o(1/c′ ) ≤ t ≤ c′ x2I /3; these cover

A numerical illustration [numerical]. At c = 128, 256, 512, 1024 the smallest sampled
value of g, in units of h0 at the middle of the same cell, lies between +0.065 and +0.080; it is
attained at the first pair of detuned sites, and deep in the band the same ratio is about 0.35. Also
Band/|Lobe| ∈ [0.074, 0.081]. The proof uses none of these numbers. At c = 256 the functional
would give ∆1 ≤ a + t⋆ + 1 = 32.3, against the numerical optimum 29.72 of table 2.
    Three things are not claimed: that the optimal functional has this form; that 0.483 is the
best constant in (7.5); and anything at a specific finite c.


8     From functionals to CFT spectra
Theorem 5 supplies a feasible function in Ā. That such a function constrains every conformal
field theory is Theorem 2, which we prove in this section, without a computer; Corollary 6 then
follows at once.

8.1   The issue, the idea, and the statement
The sum rule (2.5) came from applying a functional to the crossing equation term by term.
For a functional of finite order with compact support in β this is termwise differentiation of a
series of exponentials that converges uniformly with all its derivatives. A function g ∈ Ā is only
continuous and integrable against the Cardy weight. If it comes from a functional ω at all, the
support of ω may reach the boundary of moduli space, β → 0 and β → ∞, and exchanging ω
with the sum over primaries is then a genuine condition, the analogue of the swapping conditions
                                                                               P
for four-point functions [35, 36, 68]. Two things can go wrong. First, n dn g(tn ) need not
converge absolutely: the primaries can sit anywhere, their number grows like the Cardy density,
and M [g] < ∞ controls |g| only in the mean. Second, the vacuum sits at t = −a, that is, at
b = ±i/2 in the variable of section 2.5, off the real axis, whereR g is not given but defined by the
eigen-identity (Definition 2.5); one must show that gvac = − g ρC so defined is what crossing
assigns to the vacuum.

The idea. We never construct ω. We expand g in the Laguerre functions ψk of (2.10). Because
ĝ = −g only odd k occur, and each odd ψk is a derivative functional of finite order at β = 1,
which annihilates the crossing equation. The Laguerre series of g cannot be summed against the
spectrum term by term, because its partial sums need not converge there. We therefore insert
                                                                   P
a factor rk with 0 < r < 1 and consider the Abel mean Abr g = k rk gk ψk , where gk are the


                                                 50
Laguerre coefficients of g. On the crossing kernel this regulator is a contraction of the modulus
toward the self-dual point: Abr Ξβ is a multiple of Ξβr , where ϖ(βr ) = r ϖ(β) in the variable
ϖ := (β − 1)/(β + 1), so that βr stays in the compact interval [ 1−r     1+r
                                                                   1+r , 1−r ]. Each Abel mean thus
behaves as a functional supported strictly inside moduli space, and the sum rule holds for Abr g
at every r < 1 at no cost beyond Ẑ(β) < ∞.
    The content of the theorem is the removal of the regulator, r ↑ 1, which lets the support
reach the cusps where the Cardy growth lives. The Abel mean is an integral operator with a
positive, symmetric kernel, known in closed form, which concentrates on the diagonal as r ↑ 1.
The vacuum enters through its S-image, the Cardy continuum on t ≥ 0, and symmetry moves
Abr from g onto ρC . The kernel preserves the Cardy growth of ρC up to a constant factor,
uniformly in r (Lemma 8.1), so M [g] < ∞R is exactly the domination that dominated convergence
requires, and the vacuum side tends to g ρC = −gvac . On the primary side no domination is
available. Feasibility supplies a sign instead, and for non-negative terms Fatou’s lemma needs
                                                                          P                     P
none: the sum of the lower limits is at most the lower limit of the sum, n lim inf r ≤ lim inf r n .
It applies to the positive part g+ . The negative part g− , the lobe, lives on [0, T ], below every
primary by hypothesis, and its leakage onto the primaries, a Gaussian tail of the kernel, vanishes
                                                                    P
against the Cardy growth of their number. The outcome is 0 ≤ n dn g(tn ) ≤ −gvac < 0.

The statement. This proves Theorem 2: for c ≥ 40, if g ∈ Ā is feasible at some T > 0, then
no admissible spectrum (Definition 2.1) has all its primaries at tn ≥ T . The argument is by
contradiction from tn ≥ T > 0 for all n, so light primaries never enter it, while in the conclusion
the primary with tn < T may be a light one. The hypothesis that there are no conserved currents
beyond Virasoro (section 2.1) cannot be dropped within this framework: a current J enters
every sum rule as dJ [g(tJ ) − g(tJ + 1)], a difference whose sign feasibility does not control.

8.2   Set-up
We list what section 8.3 uses, so that the proof can be checked line by line. Classical facts about
Laguerre polynomials are quoted from [69]; everything else is proved.
                                                                  √
Conventions. Throughout this section u := 4πt and σ := u and likewise for u′ ; a function
of t ≥ 0 is regarded as a function of u without change of name, and Lp (du) refers to [0, ∞).
Thus ψk = Lk (u)e−u/2 . We write eβ (t) := e−2πβt = e−βu/2 , so that Ξβ = eβ − β −1 e1/β by (2.4);
r ∈ (0, 1) is the Abel ratio. For a spectrum {(tn , dn )} with all tn ≥ 0 the signed spectral measure
and its pairing with a function f on [0, ∞) are
                      X                                      X                  Z ∞
               m :=       dn δtn − ρC (t) dt,   ⟨m, f ⟩ :=       dn f (tn ) −         ρC f dt,   (8.1)
                      n                                      n                  0


and R|m| is the same measure with a plus sign. By (2.12) the vacuum term of Ẑ(β) equals
β −1 0∞ ρC e1/β dt, so the crossing equation (2.3) reads ⟨m, Ξβ ⟩ = 0: the vacuum has been traded
for its S-image, and only t ≥ 0 occurs. Growth and decay are measured by the weights
                                                       √
       Wq,ℓ (t) := (1 + u)q eℓσ (q, ℓ ∈ R),     ℓa := 2 πa,       0 ≤ ρC ≤ Cρ W−1/4,ℓa .     (8.2)

                           ′
                                                                 √ on ρC , with Cρ = Cρ (a),
follows from ρC = 2πV ≤ 4πKa and Ka = I0 (ℓa σ) ≤ 1.18 eℓa σ / 1 + ℓa σ (Lemma B.4(c));
ρC ≥ 0 by Lemma B.2(a). Since c is fixed, constants may depend on a; what matters is
uniformity in r.

Five facts about the spectrum. Let {(tn , dn )} be an admissible spectrum with all tn ≥ 0,
             P
and NZ (R) := tn ≤R dn its counting function.

The kernel on weighted spaces. The proof needs Abr to be bounded uniformly in r, and
to tend to the identity, on functions that grow or decay like the Cardy factor. These are (K1)
and (K3) of the next lemma; (K2) serves only to prove (K3). The proof is a Gaussian estimate
and can be skipped at first reading.

need it.

8.4      Consequences
By Theorem 2, ∆1 ≤ TV (c) + a for every admissible CFT with c ≥ 40: the quantity that
Theorem 4 bounds from below is a bound on theories, not only the value of a linear program.
The two halves of Theorem 1 still say different things: attainment, with Theorem 2, is a statement
about the spectra of actual theories, while optimality is a statement about the reach of the
method—no functional in Ā does better—and says nothing about the existence of theories with
a gap near ∆best
             1 .




                                                        55
                                                                        1
Corollary 6 (proved, computer-assisted). For c ≥ catt
                                                   0 the function g = 2 (h0 − ĥ0 ) of Theorem 5
is feasible at t⋆ + 1. Hence every admissible CFT with central charge c ≥ catt
                                                                           0 has

                                            c
                    ∆1 < a + t⋆ + 1 =         + A1 c1/3 + O(1),       A1 = 0.483.              (8.16)
                                           ΛV

Proof. Theorem 5 states that g ∈ Ā and that g is feasible at t⋆ + 1; Theorem 2, which
applies because catt0  ≥ 40, then gives a primary with tn < t⋆ + 1. By Theorem 5, t⋆ =
ξ 1 c + A1 c
  ∗  ′      ′1/3 1 + O(c′−2/3 ) , and a + ξ1∗ c′ = c′ /ΛV by the definition of ΛV in Theorem 1;
replacing c = c − 1 by c changes the right side by O(1).
             ′


     The tag is inherited from Theorem 5; the step from the functional to theories is by hand.
The coefficient 0.483 belongs to one explicit construction and is not claimed to be the best;
Conjecture 1 predicts Aopt = 0.27666 for ∆best                                         att
                                               1 . Nothing is said about the size of c0 or about
finite c: at every central charge accessible to numerical work (8.16) is weaker than the numerical
bounds (section 7.10). What Corollary 6 adds is that the slope ΛV is reached by a bound on
actual theories.

Remark (the case gvac = 0; proved). Only the last step of (8.15) used gvac > 0. If g ∈ Ā is
                                                           P
weakly feasible at T > 0 with gvac = 0, steps (i)–(iv) give n dn g(tn ) ≤ 0 for every admissible
spectrum with all tn ≥ T ; since each term is non-negative, such a spectrum is supported on the
zeros of g in [T, ∞). This is the extremality statement of section 2.2, and by itself it bounds
nothing.


9      A second route to attainment: one transform of one real
       function
Theorem 5 attains the slope with a functional g = 12 (h0 − ĥ0 ), where h0 is a product whose
zeros sit at the quantiles of the zero law ρU . It is positive on the band because ĥ0 is smaller
there than h0 itself; this, with a similar inequality for the vacuum value, is where a computer
certifies finitely many inequalities. That proof gives a rate, TV (c) ≤ ξ1∗ c′ + 0.483 c′1/3 + O(1),
and it shows how the extremal potential U is a blueprint for a functional.
    This section describes a second, quite different way to build a functional that certifies
∆1 ≤ c/ΛV + o(c). It gives less and costs much less. Its building block is minus the imaginary
part of a rotation of phase space by 45◦ applied to one real, positive function: |r′ |2 times a bell
that is a product of two double sine functions. The functional itself is the same transform of one
real function, a combination of a few such blocks and of Gaussians. Its construction needs no
positivity on an oscillating band and no computer. It gives the slope ΛV but no rate, and it is
far from the optimal functional. Nothing outside this section depends on it.
    Result S1
    For every xg > x∗1 there is c0 (xg ) ≥ 40 such that for every c ≥ c0 (xg ) there is a functional
    g ∈ Ā that is feasible at T = c′ x2g /3. By Theorem 2 every admissible CFT with c ≥
    c0 (xg ) then has ∆1 < a + c′ x2g /3. Hence lim supc→∞ TV (c)/c′ ≤ ξ1∗ , and with Theorem 4,
    limc→∞ c/∆best1 (c) = ΛV . No computer is used.

What is not obtained: a rate (the statement is o(c) only), a value of c0 (xg ), and any information
about the optimal functional. The strategy has three steps.
 (i) Every real function gives a functional (section 9.1). The Fourier transform is a quarter-turn
     of phase space (section 2.3). Let R be the eighth-turn, its square root. For every real radial
     function ϕ the imaginary part of R[ϕ] is mapped to minus itself by the Fourier transform.
     So there is no constraint left to solve, and all the content is in the choice of ϕ.

                                                  56
 (ii) A seed whose image is a bump (sections 9.2 and 9.3). We choose for ϕ the factor |r′ |2 times
      a positive bell with a Gaussian core and exponential tails. At large c its image gγ is a
      bump: it is negative on a window that contains its peak, its Cardy-weighted size |gγ |V is
      concentrated at exponential order around one point xγ , its vacuum value is positive and
      of the same exponential order as the peak, and beyond the peak it decays faster than
      the Cardy weight grows. A parameter γ ∈ (0, π) moves the peak over the whole interval
      x∗1 < xγ < 12 between the optimal gap bound and Hellerman’s point. In terms of t the bump
      at central charge c is the critical member γ = π of the family at the larger central charge
      c′γ = (π/γ)2 c′ ; read against the weaker Cardy growth of the lighter theory, it acquires a
      margin.
(iii) From bumps to a feasible functional (section 9.4). One bump with its peak just above x∗1
      already has the right vacuum value and a negative lobe in the right place. Beyond xg it is
      exponentially small compared with the inverse Cardy weight, but its sign is not known.
      We add, with an exponentially small coefficient, the negatives of bumps whose peaks cover
      [xg , 12 ]: they form a positive plateau that lies just below the Cardy line and dominates
      everything of unknown sign. Beyond x = 12 the crossing kernels Ξβ with β < 1 do the same
      job.

Level of rigor. The aim is to explain the strategy and the functional. Exact statements are
marked as such. The large-c statements are leading-order saddle-point estimates; we state what
they give and why, without uniform bounds on the remainders. For this reason Result S1 and
Result S2 do not carry the tag [proved] of section 1.3, and the status of Theorem 1, Theorem 5
and Corollary 6 does not rest on this section.
                                                                                p
Notation. It is that of section 2: t = |r|2 /2 and b = x + iy = 3t/c′ (2.17); a functional is
a real radial function g of r ∈ R2 , written as a function of t, with ĝ = −g for the transform
                                                                                       ′
(2.9); ψk are the Laguerre functions (2.10); the Cardy weight      R∞

and ρC = 2πV ; for g ∈ Ā the vacuum value is gvac = − 0 g ρC dt (Definition 2.5), and
feasibility is as in Definition 2.3; Ξβ is the crossing kernel (2.4). The extremal potential U and
U0 = U (0) = −0.388748 are those of Lemma 6.5, with U ′ (x) = − 43 arcsin sinh πx on |x| < x∗1 .
The letters γ, b, Q, G, s, w, σ, η, K are local to this section.

Every real radial function ϕ gives a functional Ω[ϕ]. In words, Ω discards the even Laguerre
components of the seed, which the Fourier transform leaves alone, and keeps the odd ones with
alternating signs. Only the part 12 (ϕ − ϕ̂) of the seed matters, and a seed with ϕ̂ = ϕ, for instance
e−2πt , gives zero.

The sum rule, seen directly. Apply (9.3)            to the reduced partition function (2.2) term by

Ẑ(β) = β −1                that β 1/2 Ẑ(β) is real. Since β(τ ) = i(τ − i)/(τ + i), the coefficient

in (9.3) is 2/(1 + τ 2 ) β , a positive multiple of the principal square root of β. So R of a
real superposition of Gaussians, applied to Ẑ, is real provided the superposition converges on
Ẑ, and its imaginary part vanishes: this is the sum rule for Ω[ϕ]. The bell G of section 9.2 is
                                         ′
a superposition of Gaussians e−2πτ t , τ > 0, with positive weights (the product over its zeros
and poles shows that G is a completely monotone function of r2 ), and the factor |r′ |2 = 2t′ of
the seed is −π −1 ∂τ acting on them. So the seed is a real superposition, which converges on Ẑ
for γ < π, and its functional acts on Ẑ along the unit semicircle in the β-plane, which is the
set fixed by S followed by complex conjugation. The crossing kernels are of this form too: for
0 < β < 1, Ξβ is a multiple of Ω applied to the real part of a complex Gaussian. In the proof
of Result S1 we do not use this paragraph; Theorem 2 handles the sum rule for every feasible
g ∈ Ā.

Per unit of vacuum cost, the kernel Ξ1/2x′ delivers the Cardy rate at x = x′ , with the positive
sign. But it does so only for x′ > 12 , because Ξβ with β > 1 is negative at large t. This is why
a positive plateau at the Cardy rate can be made of crossing kernels with real β only beyond
Hellerman’s point x = 12 , that is, ∆ ≈ c/6; the bumps reach the Cardy rate on the remaining
interval (x∗1 , 12 ).
    Consequently, let |f (c′ x2 /3)| ≤ e−c [L(x)+η ] for x ≥ xa ≥ 12 , with the extra factor e−c σ(x−xb ) ,

The functional. Fix xg ∈ (x∗1 , 12 ). The map γ 7→ xγ is a decreasing bijection of (0, π) onto
(x∗1 , 12 ).
 (1) Main bump. Choose γ0 with x∗1 < xγ0 < xg . By (9.12) and (B2), used with x̄ between xγ0
     and min(xg , xt ), there is η1 > 0 with |ḡγ0 | ≤ e−c [L(x)+η1 −o(1)] for x ≥ xg .
                                                          ′


 (2) Margins. Let γg be the value of γ with xγ = xg , and let δ be a third of the distance xt − xγ
     at γg . This distance grows as γ decreases, so δ ≤ 31 (xt − xγ ) for every bump with its peak
     in [xg , 21 ). Let K bound 12 |(Uγ + L)′′ | on the windows [xmin , xγ + δ] of all these bumps; it
     is finite because (Uγ + L)′′ depends on γx only, and γx ≤ γg (xg + δ) < arcsinh 1 there.
     Choose a mesh ϵ < δ/2 and η > 0 with η + Kϵ2 < min(η1 , ηδ ).
 (3) Plateau. Choose γ1 > · · · > γN whose peaks x(k) := xγk form a grid of [xg , 12 ) of mesh at
     most ϵ, with x(1) ≤ xg + ϵ and x(N ) ≥ 12 − ϵ.
 (4) Repairs. Let R0 be a repair of ḡγ0 on x ≥ 12 , and Rk a repair of ḡγk on x ≥ max( 12 , x(k) + δ);
     what a repair needs is supplied by step (1), by (9.17) and by the remark after it.
All these choices are made before c → ∞; for the repairs this means their nodes, hence tR , while
their coefficients depend on c. The functional is the flat sum

                                                     62
9.5    Remarks
Comparison with the functional of Theorem 5. The functional of Theorem 5 is built
to have the profile U : its zeros are placed by the zero law, it runs along the Cardy line on the
band, and the difficulty is to show that the oscillating function h0 − ĥ0 stays positive there. The
functional (9.19) avoids the band altogether. On [xg , 12 ] it lies below the Cardy line by the fixed
margin η (figure 9(b)), so it is positive for a crude reason, and it is far from extremal. The price
is that the margins η1 , ηδ , η go to zero as xg → x∗1 , while the estimates hold for c′ η ≫ 1; this is
why no rate comes out. The two constructions share the potential U and nothing else. A third
construction, in Mellin space, is sketched in appendix A.

Consistency with the optimality theorem. Theorem 3 forbids a function in Ā whose
Cardy-weighted mass sits below x∗1 . Accordingly the peaks obey xγ > x∗1 . At γ = π the peak,
the turning point and x∗1 coincide, and to leading order the tail falls at the Cardy rate: |gπ |V is
flat at exponential order along the whole band, and the critical member has no margin. (It is
still in Ā: the first pole of G is at Im r = Q/4 and not at 1/4b, so its tail beats the Cardy weight
by the factor e−πx/2 , which does not depend on c; M [gγ ] = ∞ only for γ > π(1 + b2 ).) For fixed
γ > π the tail is slower than the Cardy rate. Taking γ < π is what creates the margins, and by
(9.13) it amounts to borrowing the critical function of a heavier theory.

Why this seed [heuristic]. On WKB data a function that the Fourier transform maps to ±
itself lives on a curve invariant under the quarter-turn, and the relevant one is sinh γb = i sinh γp.
It is exponential in both b and p, and an exponential of a momentum is a finite shift, so its
quantization √  is a difference equation
                                    √    rather than a differential one. In
                                                                         √ the rotated
                                                                                    √ coordinates
s = (b − p)/ 2, ps = (b + p)/ 2 the curve is a graph, ps = −(i/ 2γ) gd( 2γs), with the
momentum normalized as in (4.3): this is the saddle-point relation (9.15) read backwards, and
c′ hγ is the corresponding WKB exponent. On a graph the quantized equation is of first order,
and its normalizable solution is G: a first-order difference equation with trigonometric coefficients
is solved by double sine functions, as f (z + 1) = zf (z) is solved by the Gamma function. The
expansion (9.9) is its WKB form to one loop. The function G is moreover equal to its own Fourier
transform in one dimension, a special case of the Fourier transformation formula of [70, 71],
which reflects the invariance of the curve; we do not use this. So the bell G is the wave function
of the quantized, deformed spectral curve in the rotated polarization, and the bump is the same
state in the polarization of section 4. The passage to the plane, with the radius as the argument
and the factor |r′ |2 , is a recipe that works because the centrifugal term is invisible at leading
order; we have not derived it. We know of no meaning in conformal field theory of the shift
by ib, and at γ = π the parameter is not that of Liouville theory at the same central charge
(b2 = 3/4c′ , against 6/c).

Zeros. The functional (9.19) is strictly positive on [xg , 21 ]; it has no band of double zeros. If
xg → x∗1 slowly as c → ∞, it is a sequence that attains ΛV . By Theorem 7 its profile then tends
to U , and its zeros in the strip have the density c′ ρU within any fixed distance of the slits. These
zeros lie off the real axis; we have not located them.


10     What near-optimal functionals must look like
This section is proved throughout, and no computer enters. It uses Proposition 6.4, Lemma 6.5,
Definition 6.6 and Lemma 6.7 and, only to know that attaining sequences exist, Theorem 5.
Nothing outside this section depends on it.
   Theorems 3 and 4 are inequalities at finite c: they bound what a functional can certify. Here
we ask the converse question: what must a functional look like that comes close to the bound?


                                                  63
The tool is a limit. Along a sequence gj ∈ Ā at central charges cj → ∞ the profiles ugj are
functions on one and the same strip S, which does not depend on c. If the sequence attains
the slope ΛV , the limit is the extremal potential U , and the zeros of gj follow the zero law ρU
(Theorem 7).

10.1    Limits of potentials
Let g ∈ Ā \ {0}. By section 6.3, G(b) = g(c′ b2 /3) is even and holomorphic on S, and bounded
and continuous on S̄; and the profile (6.13), with its Riesz measure (6.10),

                            1      |G(b)|             1 2      1    X
                 ug (b) =      log        ,   ng =      ∇ ug = ′            mζ δζ ,          (10.1)
                            c′      M [g]            2π       c ζ∈S: G(ζ)=0

is the potential of positive point charges mζ /c′ at the zeros of G in the strip: ng is the zero-
counting measure of G divided by c′ . For a sequence we write uj := ugj and c′j := cj − 1. As in
Theoremp4, g is called weakly feasible if it is weakly feasible at some T , hence at T = t1 (g), and
x1 (g) = 3 t1 (g)/c′ as in (2.17). The idea is that the three facts on which section 6 rests pass
to the limit: limits of subharmonic functions are subharmonic; the majorant of Proposition 6.4,
whose error is O(log c′ /c′ ), becomes exact; and weak feasibility, which enters through the half-
mass condition (2.16), becomes contact with the Cardy line. The notions needed to say this are
in the box; the proofs are in appendices I.1 to I.3.

  Toolkit: limits of potentials
                                                      1
 Let uj be potentials of positive charges nj = 2π       ∇2 uj on S (subharmonic functions).
 Convergence        1
               in Lloc is convergence of the potentials in the mean on every compact set,
 R
    |u
   K j  − u| dA → 0; pointwise convergence is too much to ask, because a potential is −∞ at
 its charges, but the logarithm is integrable in two dimensions. Vague convergence nj → n is
 convergence of the charge distributions when tested against continuous functions of compact
 support; then nj (K) → n(K) for every compact K whose boundary carries no charge of n.
 Compactness [72, Thms. 3.2.12–3.2.13]: potentials that are bounded above on compact sets,
 uniformly in j, and do not run off to −∞ carry a bounded charge on every compact set, and
 along a subsequence potentials and charges both converge. Upper semicontinuity: at a point
 a limit potential can jump up, never down; the form that we use is (I.1). Superharmonic,
 strong minimum principle, circle means: θ is superharmonic if −θ is subharmonic; on a con-
 nected open set a non-negative superharmonic function is identically zero or strictly positive,
 and its mean over a circle is at most its value at the center [72, §3.2], [63, Thm. 2.3.1].

Lemma 10.1 (Compactness; proved). Let cj → ∞ and gj ∈ Ā \ {0}. After passing to a
subsequence, either (i) ugj → −∞ uniformly on every compact subset of S, or (ii) ugj → u in
                                                              1
L1loc (S) for a subharmonic function u ̸≡ −∞ on S, and ngj → 2π ∇2 u vaguely on S.

A function u that arises in (ii) is called a limit profile of the sequence.

Lemma 10.2 (Majorant; proved). Every limit profile satisfies u ≤ U on all of S, where U is
extended across the slits as in Definition 6.6: it is continuous, equal to −L on the slits, and
subharmonic with charge density ρU (x) dx there. In particular u ≤ 0 on S, and u ≤ −L on the
real axis.

Proposition 10.3 (Contact; proved). Let cj → ∞, and let gj ∈ Ā \ {0} be weakly feasible with
x1 (gj ) → x∞ < ∞. Then alternative (i) of Lemma 10.1 does not occur, and every limit profile u
has a contact point x0 ∈ [x∗1 , x∞ ], at which u(x0 ) = U (x0 ) = −L(x0 ). In particular x∞ ≥ x∗1 ,
which is the qualitative statement of Theorem 4 once more.


                                                 64
    In the dictionary of section 6.2: a limit profile lies below the extremal potential, and the last
sign change of the functionals leaves a mark on it, a point not beyond x∞ at which the profile
touches the Cardy line; by Lemma 6.7 this point lies on a slit.

10.2     Rigidity of the zeros
Theorem 7 (Rigidity of the zero law; proved). Let cj → ∞, and let gj ∈ Ā \ {0} be weakly
feasible and attain the slope ΛV , that is, x1 (gj ) → x∗1 . (By Proposition 10.3 it suffices that
lim supj x1 (gj ) ≤ x∗1 ; such sequences exist by Theorem 5.) Then:
 (a) ugj → U in L1loc (S), along the whole sequence.
 (b) The normalized zero-counting measures converge vaguely on S to the zero law ρU (x) =
      4
     3π arccosh(sinh π|x|) of Definition 6.6, carried by the slits:

                                    1 X
                            ngj =         mζ δζ −→ ρU (x) dx ⊗ δy=0                   on |x| ≥ x∗1 .            (10.2)
                                    c′j ζ

     Concretely, counting zeros with multiplicity: in every compact subset of S disjoint from the
     slits Gj has o(c′j ) zeros, and for x∗1 < xa < xb and 0 < y0 < 21

                      # zeros of Gj in [xa , xb ] × [−y0 , y0 ]       = c′j          ρU (x) dx + o(c′j ).       (10.3)

                                                                                               R
     under b 7→ c′j b2 /3 (a lens |Im t| ≤ 23 c′j xb y0 around [c′j x2a /3, c′j x2b /3]) is c′j ξξab 2ρV (ξ) dξ+o(c′j ),
     where ξ = x2 /3, ξa = x2a /3, ξb = x2b /3, and ρV = 3ρU /(4x) is the band density of (4.16).
     In words, near [t, t + dt] there are 2ρV (t/c′ ) dt zeros.

    Item (c) reproduces, counted with multiplicity, the density of the band of double zeros of
Conjecture 1(b), and item (a) is the profile stated there, in the mean. Of what section 4 derived
from the hypotheses (W), (A), (B) and (Sel), the band density and the profile thus follow, at
leading order and in the weak sense of (a) and (b), from attainment alone; the finer statements
of Conjecture 1 are not obtained in this way. The hypotheses themselves, which concern the
semiclassical sheets of the optimal functional, are not thereby derived. The theorem also turns
the blueprint of section 7.1 into a necessity.
What the theorem does not say. It does not say that the zeros near the band are real, or
that they come in pairs; whether an attaining functional must oscillate there, as the numerical
optima do, is not decided. It says nothing about individual positions, the Airy layer at the tip,
the boundary layer at the vacuum, the phase of the comb, zeros on the edges or outside the
closed strip, or rates.
Idea of the proof (appendix I.4). Let u be a limit profile. By Lemma 10.2 the deficit
θ := U − u is non-negative; it is superharmonic on the slit strip D, where U is harmonic; and by
Proposition 10.3 with x∞ = x∗1 it vanishes at the tip x∗1 , a boundary point of D. By the strong
minimum principle either θ ≡ 0 on D, which is the claim, or θ > 0 on D. Had the contact point
been inside D, this would end the proof; at a point of the open slit it would not; the tip is the
borderline case, and it is decided by a competition of exponents. If θ > 0 on D, then near the
tip θ dominates a multiple of the harmonic measure of an arc in a disk slit along a radius. The
square-root map that opens the slit shows that this harmonic measure vanishes at the tip only
like the square root of the distance, so the mean of θ over a circle of radius ϵ about the tip is
≳ ϵ1/2 . On the other hand u is subharmonic across the slit and u(x∗1 ) = U (x∗1 ), so the circle
means of θ are at most those of U − U (x∗1 ),
                                           p
                                              which the Poisson–Jensen formula expresses through
the charge of U near the tip. Since ρU ∼ x − x∗1 , they are at most Cϵ3/2 . For small ϵ the two
bounds contradict each other: the tip decides.

                                                          65
Part V        Outlook

11     Discussion
11.1    Theorem, conjecture, open
In terms of the legend of section 1.3, the results stand as follows. Proved: the slope ΛV (Theo-
rem 1), in its two halves, optimality with a rate (Theorems 3 and 4) and attainment (Theorem 5);
and certification (Theorem 2), which turns attainment into a bound on conformal field theories
(Corollary 6). Only attainment and what rests on it, that is, the second half of Theorem 1,
Theorem 5 and Corollary 6, use computer assistance. Also proved, without a computer, is the
rigidity of the zero law (Theorem 7). Conjectured: everything finer (Conjecture 1), in particular
the coefficient Aopt = 0.27666 of c1/3 , the profile of the optimal functional, and the offset c/24
in the positions of its zeros (seen numerically in [10, 11]; section 4.6). Explained but not proved:
the origin of the earlier extrapolation to the slope 9.08 [11] (section 5.2).

11.2    Three-dimensional gravity; Virasoro versus U (1)c
Invariance under S at zero angular potential, used through functionals in Ā, certifies ∆1 ≲
c/9.1266 at large c and no more: the window (1.3) is closed to it. This is a statement about the
method. It neither supports nor threatens pure gravity [3], and it does not say which spectra
with ∆1 in the window are realized. To reach the BTZ threshold one needs spin [8, 23, 24],
integrality of the degeneracies [25, 26], or data from correlation functions. Arguments through
the elliptic point of order three, τ = e2πi/3 , which is fixed not by S but by ST and so involves
spin [73–76], aim at the BTZ threshold itself; the present results neither use them nor bear on
them. The same holds for the bound ∆1 ≤ (c − 1)/12 + 0.23 on what [76] calls the spinless gap:
it rests on the kernel of ST 1 S, and so on T as well as S, and it concerns spectra in which every
non-vacuum primary has spin zero, whereas here primaries of every spin are counted.

Virasoro versus U (1)c . The two problems have the same architecture and different numbers,
9.1266 against π 2 . The slope π 2 is proved in [13, Ch. 1]; we have read the statements and the
structure of that argument but have not independently verified it; nothing in this paper depends
on it logically. That proof works on a strip in the Mellin variable, in dimension d = 2c → ∞.
Here the dimension d = 2 is fixed, the strip is in the position-like variable b, and c enters through
the position of the vacuum; the version in the Mellin variable, where the strip becomes a domain
with a free boundary, is sketched in appendix A. There is no analogue here of the condition
f (0) = 0 on the functional of section 2.3, which the U (1)c proof uses to cancel a pole of the
Mellin transform on the edge of the strip and to make the integral of the functional vanish. Two
different objects should be kept apart. The exact optimizers of both problems, as computed
numerically, have a band of double zeros (sections 3.1 and 4.6), whereas the asymptotically
attaining functions constructed in [13, Ch. 1, Thm. 4.1] are free of real zeros on the whole
constrained region. For Virasoro a functional that attains ΛV must carry zeros at density c′ ρU
near the real axis, by Theorem 7, though not necessarily on it; this freedom is what the detuning
of section 7 exploits.

Note added. After this paper was completed, Chang, Liu and Ma [77] constructed modular-
invariant torus partition functions with a unique vacuum, integer spins and positive integer
degeneracies whose gap is ∆1 = (1 + κ)(c − 1)/12, for small fixed κ > 0 at every sufficiently
large c. These are partition functions, not full conformal field theories. We have not verified this
construction; if it stands, the lower end of the window (1.3) passes every constraint of the torus
partition function, spin and integrality included, so that Λ < 12 for every bound that uses this
function alone, and reaching the BTZ threshold requires data beyond it. Perlmutter [78] has

                                                 66
shown, for theories whose spectral counting fluctuations are logarithmically rigid, that ∆1 −
(c − 1)/12 ≤ o(c), apart from at most one exceptional value of the scalar gap at each sufficiently
large c. Salmon [79] has determined the asymptotic rate of Delsarte’s linear programming bound
for binary and spherical codes, with lower bounds from potential theory through an obstacle
problem solved in closed form, as in appendix A.

11.3    Open problems
Effective constants. More useful than the values of copt0  and catt
                                                                  0 , which are explicit in principle
and not computed, would be certificates in interval arithmetic of the explicit functional at fixed c.
    The coefficient of c1/3 . A proof of Aopt needs the comb with no site omitted (the construction
of section 7 omits the first site of the zero law), the Airy layer at the tips, and the balance
between lobe and band in the vacuum inequality. Apply the edge representation of section 7.4
to a numerically optimal functional, whose transform is ĝ = −g. It suggests that on the band ĝ
is the sum of two parts. One oscillates, with relative amplitude 12 and in antiphase with the
oscillation of g, so that the zeros are doubled rather than lifted off the axis. The other is a mean,
which is manufactured near the vacuum point of the edge; the construction of section 7 does not
attempt this. A proof of Aopt would have to control both. We do not claim it.
    Further problems. To remove the logarithm in Theorem 4, that is, to prove t1 (g) ≥ ξ1∗ c′ −
O(c1/3 ), the scale of Conjecture 1. To find a convex dual of the linear program of section 2.2
with the extremal potential U as its value function, which would explain why the potential of
section 6 is the curve of section 4. To bound TV (c) from below at a given c: numerics bound it
from above only (section 2.6), and Theorem 4 starts at copt 0 . To derive from the linear program
the selection rule (Sel) of section 4.4, on which Conjecture 1 rests. To find what replaces the
extremal problem of section 6 in the bootstrap with spin [8, 23].


A      Mellin space approach
                                                                p
The proofs of sections 6 and 7 work in the variable b = 3t/c′ , in which a functional is
holomorphic on a strip. This appendix sketches a second derivation of the slope, Theorem 1, in
which a functional is studied through its Mellin transform. It gives both halves: no functional in
Ā certifies more than ΛV , and functionals exist that come arbitrarily close. The Mellin transform
is the tool of the proof for the U (1)c problem in [13, Ch. 1], where the transform of a functional
is controlled on a strip. The attaining functions of [13, Ch. 1, Thm. 4.1] are likewise built in
Mellin space, by multiplying the Mellin transform of a Gaussian by factors that respect the
Fourier symmetry and reading the signs from saddle points, and the functional of appendix A.6
has this form, with a symbol whose zeros come from the solution of the free-boundary problem
described below. (The Mellin transform of the auxiliary functions of the sphere-packing linear
program, with its functional equation and its relation to their Taylor coefficients, appears in
Cohn and Miller [80, §5], for smooth and rapidly decreasing radial functions in any dimension.
Properties (i) and (ii) of (A.3) follow from their formulas in dimensionR two with fˆ = −f when
f (x) = g(x2 /2) is smooth and rapidly decreasing: with Mf (s) = 0∞ f (x) xs−1 dx one has
mg (z) = 2π z Mf (2z)/Γ(z).) What is new for Virasoro is that the vacuum sits at t = −a < 0, off
the half line over which the transform is taken. It then enters through all the integer moments
of g at once, and the problem on a strip becomes a free-boundary problem in the plane: an
obstacle problem, which we solve in closed form. The curve sinh πb = i sinh πp appears once
more, now as the solution of the free-boundary condition, and the threshold x∗1 is fixed by the
point of the contact set that is nearest to the axis of the reflection z → 1 − z, which is what the
Fourier transform becomes.
    What this appendix has in common with the main text is not repeated. The problem, the
class Ā and the vacuum value are those of section 2, and a feasible g ∈ Ā bounds every conformal


                                                 67
field theory by Theorem 2. The two results are the following.
  Result M1 (optimality)

  For every δ ∈ (0, x∗1 ) there is Cδ < ∞ such that for all c ≥ 40 and every g ∈ Ā, with no
  condition on the sign of g,
      Z c′ (x∗ −δ)2 /3
             1                                                                         
                         |g| V dt ≤ Cδ c′ 4 e−κM (δ) c M [g],
                                                     ′
                                                                κM (δ) := W π3 (x∗1 − δ) > 0,   (A.1)
       0

 where W is the explicit function of appendix A.3. Consequently, by the half-mass condition
 (2.16), for every xT < x∗1 there is c0 (xT ) such that for c ≥ c0 (xT ) no g ∈ Ā other than zero
 is weakly feasible at T = c′ x2T /3. No computer is used.

  Result M2 (attainability)

  For every xg > x∗1 there is c0 (xg ) ≥ 40 such that for every real c ≥ c0 (xg ) there is √gc ∈ Ā
  with gc > 0 on [c′ x2g /3, ∞) and (gc )vac > 0; moreover gc decays faster than e−4π αt for
  every α > 0. So gc is feasible at T = c′ x2g /3, and by Theorem 2 every admissible CFT with
  c ≥ c0 (xg ) has ∆1 < a + c′ x2g /3.

Together they give limc→∞ TV (c)/c′ = ξ1∗ , that is, Theorem 1, once more. Result M1 is Theorem 3
with a smaller exponent, κM (δ) ≃ 3.73 δ 2 against κ(δ) ≃ 2.65 δ 3/2 (appendix A.4), and it contains
the first statement of Theorem 4. Result M2 is Result S1 of section 9, by a third construction.
Neither gives a rate, and the central charges c0 are not explicit.
    The plan is as follows. Appendix A.1 recasts the constraints in Mellin space: the Fourier
transform becomes a reflection, and the vacuum becomes a series over the integers with positive
coefficients, each of which bounds the transform on a vertical line. Appendix A.2 takes the
scaling limit of these bounds, the obstacle, and explains why the threshold is the tip of the
set where the largest subharmonic function below the obstacle touches it. Appendix A.3 finds
this set and this function in closed form, and appendix A.4 turns the heuristic argument into
Result M1. Appendices A.5 to A.7 construct the functionals of Result M2: the zeros of the
Mellin transform are placed along four arcs inside the contact set, the functional is recovered by
Mellin inversion, and its signs come from a saddle-point analysis in which one real saddle point
dominates. Appendix A.8 is a dictionary between the Mellin plane and the strip of section 6.

Level of rigor. The results of this appendix have been verified rigorously, but the write-up
is informal: the steps are stated and the estimates behind them are omitted. For this reason
Result M1, Result M2 and Result M3 carry no status tag. One step, the univalence of a conformal
map (appendix A.5), uses five inequalities that were checked in interval arithmetic [61]; they are
listed there and are not part of table 4. Nothing outside this appendix depends on it.
                                                                                                p

are those of Lemma 6.5 and Definition 6.6. The Mellin variable is z, and ω = µ + iν is the
rescaled variable of (A.8); in this appendix ω is not the linear functional of section 2.2. The
letters mg , θn , σ, ϑ, τ , η, ηv , ι, λ and the calligraphic letters B, U, W, K, N , E, C, H are local
to this appendix. C stands for constants that do not depend on c, on t or on the point ω.

For (i), the Laplace transform F (β) := 0                  R

and (2.8). For 0 < Re z < 1 its Mellin transform in β is 0∞ F (β)β z−1 dβ = mg (1 − z)/(2 sin πz),

The division by Γ is what makes the symbol entire: in Mellin space the Fourier transform is
the reflection z → 1 − z, and a functional is an entire function that is odd under it. The first
formula in (ii) is the definition; the second is the Taylor series of g at t = 0, where g is analytic
by Lemma B.1, read through (i). In particular mg (0) = g(0). Item (iii) is the trivial bound
together with (i).

Positivity beyond T and the vacuum condition thus bound the symbol, on all the lines Re z = n+1

point. In the strip of section 6 the vacuum is one point on an edge; here it is a bound on every
vertical line through an integer.

A.2    The obstacle
The coefficient in (A.7) has an exponential order when n and Im z are of order c. We write

                                    z = 12 + c′ ω,         ω = µ + iν,                                  (A.8)

so that z → 1 − z is ω → −ω, and use Stirling’s formula in a form that is uniform in the
imaginary part: for Re w ≥ 0, w ̸= 0, log|Γ( 12 + w)| = Re[w log w − w] + 12 log 2π − ϵ(w) with
0 ≤ ϵ(w) ≤ 12 log 2. (The remainder is minus the real part of a function that is bounded and
holomorphic in the half-plane, so it is the Poisson integral of its boundary values, which are
explicit because |Γ( 12 + is)|2 = π/ cosh πs.) The main term is elementary: for w = µ + iν with
µ > 0, Re[w log w − w] = µ log µ − µ − Dµ (ν), where

of degree one in (µ, ν), and Dµ (ν) → π2 |ν| as µ → 0+ . On the line Re z = n+1 we have n+ 12 = c′ µ.

The obstacle problem. The inequality (A.11) is not the best that one can say. Since
mg is entire, the left side is a subharmonic function of ω [63]. If B were itself subharmonic
nothing would be gained. It is subharmonic on either side of the ridge, but not across it, and a
subharmonic function cannot follow a concave ridge from below. At exponential order the left
side of (A.11) therefore lies below
                                     
                            U := sup u : u subharmonic on C, u ≤ B .                                   (A.14)

                                                     70
To find the largest subharmonic minorant of a given function is the obstacle problem [81]; in the
plane it is the equilibrium problem of weighted potential theory [82]. We use (A.14) only as a
guide to the formulas of appendix A.3: Result M3 does not assert that the function defined there
is the largest minorant, and neither of the two main arguments needs it. The general theory
says what to expect. The plane splits into the open non-contact set N = {U < B}, where U is
harmonic, since otherwise it could be increased locally, and the closed contact set K = {U = B}.
Where the obstacle is smooth, U is C 1 across the free boundary ∂K, that is, ∇U = ∇B there, and
this is the equation that determines ∂K. The solution is even in µ and in ν. The ridge belongs
to N , because the mean of B over a small circle centered on it is smaller than the value at the
center. Hence K = K+ ∪ (−K+ ) with K+ a closed subset of {µ > 0}. Finally, on K the Riesz
           1          1
measure 2π   ∇2 U is 2π ∇2 B dA = dA/(π|µ|), an area measure; this will matter in appendix A.5.

In the variables (b, q) the nonlinear condition ∇U = ∇B has become linear; this is the reason
for introducing them. The reflection ω → −ω acts as (b, q) → (iq, ib), because Υ′ is odd, so
that on −∂K+ the conditions read Re q = 0 and |Im b| = 12 : there b is on an edge of the strip
S. The reflections in the lines Re q = 0 and Re q = 12 compose to the translation q → q + 1,
those in Im b = 0 and Im b = 12 to b → b + i, which suggests a solution that is periodic in both
variables. Consider therefore the function cosh 2πb + cos 2πq of ω. It is an entire function of
(b2 , q 2 ), hence holomorphic in N . It is even, because ω → −ω exchanges its two terms. It is real
on ∂K+ by (R), since cos 2π( 12 + is) = − cosh 2πs, and hence on all of ∂N . So its imaginary
part is harmonic in N and vanishes on ∂N . Assume that it is bounded in N . Then it is a real
constant, by the Phragmén–Lindelöf principle, and the value at ω = 0, where b = q = 0, is 2:

                    cosh 2πb + cos 2πq = 2,          equivalently        sin πq = sinh πb,

Result M1 is the argument of appendix A.2 carried out at finite c. Fix xT = x∗1 − δ and

The obstacle inequality. For all c ≥ 25 and c′ |µ| ≥ 52 , pg (ω) ≤ c′ B(ω) + ka with ka =
1         2 5     1     5
2 log(32π a ) + 10 < 2 log c . What matters is that ka depends neither on g nor on ω, be-
                                ′

cause the inequality is used on all of ∂N and on entire vertical lines. It follows from (A.7) with
θn ≥ 1/2a2 uniformly in n; from Hölder’s inequality between consecutive integer moments, where
replacing a chord of the concave function n 7→ log[(4π 2 a)n /(n!)2 ] by the function costs at most
 1

The comparison step. The obstacle inequality is sharp on ∂N and wasteful inside N . Let Ω be
an open set whose closure lies in a parabolic region {µ2 ≤ A20 (1 + |ν|)} and whose boundary lies
in {c′ |µ| ≥ 52 }, and let u be continuous on the closure of Ω and harmonic in Ω, with u ≥ B on

                               pg ≤ c′ u + ka        on the closure of Ω,                           (A.26)

with the same ka . Indeed pg − c′ u − ka is subharmonic in Ω and non-positive on ∂Ω. In an
unbounded domain one needs in addition that it does not grow too fast. This is the one delicate
point, because the only bound available in the central strip is not uniform in g and c. No uniform
bound is needed: the difference grows at most like a power of |ν|, with a constant that depends
on g and c, whereas

The reason is that (A.28) integrates along the line through the saddle point of the Cardy weight,

because the right side is stationary in µ there, both sides equal U0 at x = 0, and both have the
derivative − 4π
              3 q(x). The right side is the exponent that (A.15) produces for |g(t)|/M [g] on the
line through this point, where the integrand is largest at ν = 0 because ∂ν W > 0. So that line
gives |g| V ≤ M [g] ec (U +L)+o(c ) , that is, the Cardy deficit κ(δ) itself. We have not carried this
                      ′          ′


out at finite c.

A.5    The skeleton of the contact set
A functional that attains the bound should have a symbol mc with c1′ log|mc ( 12 + c′ ω)| → U(ω)
in N . Its zeros then have the density c′ times the Riesz measure of the limit. For the limit
U itself this is the area measure dA/(π|µ|) on K, which gives no control of signs: for t ≥ T
with xT > x∗1 the line (A.16) crosses K+ , and the integrand of (A.15) has constant modulus
along the whole segment |ν| ≤ ν∗ (µ). But only the values of the limit in N enter the threshold.
Inside K the measure may therefore be rearranged, as long as the potential stays below B and is
unchanged in N . Swept together as far as possible it becomes the potential-theoretic skeleton of
K [85], a positive measure on four arcs, the creases, which issue from the tips (figure 10(a)). It
is constructed by continuing U harmonically from N across the free boundary into K+ , on a
second sheet, until the continuation meets the elementary harmonic function Re ΥR of (A.13)
along a curve.

The second sheet. By Result M3 (i), q is a conformal coordinate on N + in which the upper
arc of the free boundary is the line Re q = 12 . We move the origin to the image of the tip and put

a full neighborhood of ϑ = 0, which is the reason for using ϑ. On the negative imaginary axis

The skeleton, parametrized by its mass. The Riesz measure nsk := 2π              ∇2 Usk is carried by

The error estimates of appendix A.7 need quantitative control of ω∗ (τ ), which one obtains from
ω∗′ = iπ/ log(2q) and from the explicit far field πb ≈ ϑ. The crease does not return to the real
axis at any scale: Im ω∗ ≥ a0 (Re ω∗ − µ∗ ) with a constant a0 ∈ (0, 1], so that the creases stay out
of the sector {µ > µ∗ , |ν| < a0 (µ − µ∗ )}. Chord and arc are comparable, because arg ω∗′ ∈ (0, π2 ).
Up to a constant factor the speed |ω∗′ | decreases, but no faster than τ −1/2 , which is its rate at
the square-root tip; and |ω∗′′ | ≤ C|ω∗′ |/τ . The delicate point is the far field, where the margin in
the monotonicity of the speed is only logarithmic.

A.6    The functional
What is wanted, and what the skeleton provides. Suppose that a symbol has |mc ( 12 +
c′ ω)| = ec H(ω)+o(c ) . By Stirling’s formula the modulus of the integrand Γ(z)mc (z)(2πt)−z of
           ′        ′


the inversion integral at z = 12 + c′ (µ + iν) is
                                                                                          q
exp c′ B(µ) + H(µ) − µ log(4x2 ) + o(c′ ) × exp c′ H(µ + iν) − H(µ) − Dµ (ν) ,             x=     3t/c′ ,
                                                                                                 (A.40)
its value at the real point times its variation along the vertical line, and the weights of the vacuum
series (A.5) are e−c B(µ)+o(c ) at n = c′ µ. Thus the saddle point lies where (B + H)′ (µ) = log(4x2 );
                      ′       ′


it dominates its line if H(µ + iν) − H(µ) < Dµ (ν) for ν ̸= 0; and the vacuum series is dominated
by the terms near the maximum of H − B on the positive axis.
     The potential H∗ := Usk − U0 of the skeleton has all this, but only marginally. In the sector
free of creases it is Re ΥR plus a constant, so that the saddle point for x ≥ x∗1 is on the real axis
at µ = π3 x. By (A.37), H∗ (µ + iν) − H∗ (µ) ≤ B(µ + iν) − B(µ) = Dµ (ν) for µ ≥ µ∗ , but with
equality on the free boundary: there Re ΥII contributes a conjugate pair of saddle points, which

                                                     77
by (R) lie on the same vertical line as the real one and have the same modulus, because Usk = B
at all three points. Hence V g = r1 + r2 cos Θ(t) with a real phase Θ and positive amplitudes
r1 , r2 of the same exponential order, whose ratio is not determined at this accuracy: the sign
of g is marginal. Likewise H∗ − B = −U0 is constant on [µ∗ , ∞), so that all the terms of the
vacuum series beyond the tip, which have the wrong sign, are of the same exponential order as
the largest one.

The design. A margin is created by thinning and dilating the skeleton. Let η ∈ (0, 1), ηv > 0
and ι > 0 be three small parameters, and put

(D1) H is subharmonic, finite and C 1 on the real axis, and even in µ and in ν; its Riesz measure

The symbol. The Riesz measure of c′ H is c′ dτ along each arc. We cut every arc into cells of

There is no integrality condition, so c is an arbitrary real number. By (A.39), log|Pc | is the
midpoint rule, with step 1/c′ , for the integral c′ H. The set of zeros is invariant under ω → −ω
and ω → ω̄, so Pc is even, real on the real axis and positive there. The zero at ω = 0 makes the
symbol odd, and the pair ±µs puts its only sign change on the positive axis at the tip of the
design. What the rest of the argument uses is the following list, in which δc = C/c′ .
(P1) mc is entire, real on R, and mc (1 − z) = −mc (z).

                                                            78
(P2) log|mc ( 12 + c′ ω)| ≤ c′ [H(ω) + δc (1 + |ω|)] for all ω with µ ≥ µs , and ≥ c′ [H(ω) − δc (1 + |ω|)]
     in the corridor of (D2) and on a real interval around µv .
(P3) mc ( 12 + c′ µ) > 0 for real µ > µs , and mc (n + 1) ≤ 0 for the integers n ≥ 1 with n + 12 < c′ µs .
(P4) |Γ(z)mc (z)| ≤ Cc e−ac |Im z| for − 12 ≤ Re z ≤ 12 + c′ µa and |Im z| ≥ 1, with ac > 0.
The upper bound in (P2) is needed in the whole half-plane to the right of the tip, the arcs
included, on which the zeros lie; the lower bound only away from the zeros. (P1) and (P3) are
read off from the product. (P2) and (P4) are the subject of appendix A.7; the content is that
the error of the midpoint rule is O(1 + |ω|), with no factor c′ .

All the terms with µn < µs are non-negative by (P3). We keep the one nearest to µv ; by

Conclusion. Let xg > x∗1 . By (A.42) we choose η, ηv and ι, depending only on xg , such
that xa < 12 (x∗1 + xg ). This fixes the design, its µv and ε0 , and then all the constants above.
Since δc → 0, there is c0 (xg ) such that for c ≥ c0 (xg ) the smallness conditions on δc hold
and TH (c) ≤ c′ x2g /3. For every such real c the function gc of (A.44) lies in Ā, is positive on
[c′ x2g /3, ∞) and has (gc )vac > 0. This is Result M2.

A.7    Discretization of the skeleton
It remains to justify (P2) and (P4). In electrostatic language, c′ H is the potential of a line
charge of density c′ dτ in the mass parameter of the arcs, and log|Pc | is that of unit point charges
at the mass midpoints of the cells. The claim is that the difference is O(1), uniformly in c and
in the point of observation:

log|Pc (ω)| ≤ c′ H(ω) + C         for all ω ∈ C,             log|Pc (ω)| ≥ c′ H(ω) − C
                                                                           if dist(ω, arcs) ≥ C ℓ1 ,
                                                                                             (A.48)
where ℓj is the length of the j-th cell, and ℓ1 ≤ Cc ′−1/2 → 0. The upper bound holds everywhere,
also on the arcs, where the zeros lie; the lower bound cannot, since Pc vanishes there. For one
arc and a point w let
                                            Z                                    hj − 1 j i
                                        ′               ωj − w
                         ej (w) := c             log               dτ,    Ij =            ,        ,                (A.49)
                                            Ij         ωd (τ ) − w                 c′         c′

be the error made when the mass of the j-th cell is concentrated at its midpoint in mass. There
are three regimes. Near cells, the cell nearest to w and its two neighbors: no cancellation is
needed, because a logarithmic singularity is integrable against a mass that is spread evenly;
chord and arc being comparable and the mass per unit length being at most C/ℓj , one finds
− log(1 + ℓj /|ωj − w|) ≤ ej (w) ≤ C for every cell and every w. Far cells: ej (w) is the error of
the midpoint rule for τ 7→ log(ωd (τ ) − w); the linear term of the Taylor expansion about the
midpoint integrates to zero, and the second derivative, with |ωd′′ | ≤ C|ωd′ |/τ , gives
                                  h     1 ℓj    ℓ2j i
                   |ej (w)| ≤ C                + 2             (j ≥ 2),     dj := dist(w, cell j).                  (A.50)
                                      j − 1 dj  dj


                                                              80
The second term is the quadrupole field of the cell. The first is a residual dipole, present because
the mass midpoint of a cell is its center of mass only if the speed is constant along it. Since
the speed decreases no faster than τ −1/2 , the lengths of the cells between the j-th and the
                                                                               1
one nearest to w are comparable with ℓj in the sense that ℓj /dj ≤ C[ j−1          + k1 ], where k is the
number of cells in between, and this is square-summable, uniformly in w and c. The tip: there
           √
ωd − µs ∝ τ , and |ωd′′ | ∝ τ −3/2 is not integrable, so (A.50) has no analogue for the first cell,
however far w is from it. This is harmless, because the bound for near cells holds for every cell
and uses no second derivative; the first cell is always counted among the near ones. Summing
over the cells and over the four arcs gives (A.48).
    With the prefactor ω(ω 2 − µ2s ), which is at most (µs + |ω|)3 and is bounded below in the
corridor and near µv , (A.48) is (P2) with δc = C/c′ , for all c so large that Cℓ1 is smaller
than the distance from the arcs to the corridor and to the interval around µv . For (P4),
the dilated form of (A.37) and Dµ (ν) ≤ π2 |ν| give H(ω) ≤ (1 − η) π2 |ν| + C for |µ| ≤ µa , and
|Γ(z)| ≤ C(1 + |Im z|)Re z−1/2 e−π|Im z|/2 ; so Γ(z)mc (z) decays like e−ηπ|Im z|/2 up to a power. Here
again η > 0 is indispensable: it is the only margin against the decay of the Gamma function.

A.8    A dictionary, and remarks
The strip and the Mellin plane. The arguments of section 6 and of appendix A.4 have the
same skeleton: a holomorphic function attached to g, a bound on part of a boundary that comes
from the vacuum, the maximum principle for the logarithm of the modulus, and the half-mass
condition. They are carried out in conjugate variables. In section 6 the function is G(b) on the
strip S, and the variable is b. Here it is mg on the plane, and the variable is the action ω = 2πi
                                                                                                3 bp
(A.20), as one expects of a Mellin transform, which diagonalizes dilations. The two profiles,
ug (b) of (6.13) and the left side of (A.11), are related at exponential order by the saddle point
of the Mellin transform, that is, by a Legendre transformation in log t. The correspondence is as
follows.
 • The Fourier transform is the quarter-turn (p, b) 7→ (b, −p) of section 4 on the strip, and the
   reflection ω → −ω, which acts as (b, q) → (iq, ib), in the plane.
 • The vacuum is the point b = 2i on an edge of the strip, and gives the boundary value 0 on
   the edges (Lemma 6.1). In the plane every Taylor coefficient of the Cardy weight gives a
   bound on a vertical line; together they are the obstacle B.
 • The Cardy line −L on the slits |x| ≥ x∗1 corresponds to the obstacle on the contact set. The
   tips b = ±x∗1 of the slits are the tips ω = ± π3 x∗1 of K; on the free boundary b is real and runs
   over the band x ≥ x∗1 , and q = ip(x + i0) there. The edges |Im b| = 12 of the strip appear as
   the free boundary of −K+ .
 • The extremal potential U corresponds to U, and U (0) = U(0) = U0 (A.24). The exponents
   differ away from that point, (A.29): at the tips U + L vanishes to order 32 , and W to order 2.
 • The charge of U sits on the slits, with the density ρU of (6.21). The charge of U is the area
   measure dA/π|µ| on K, and the two have the same projection: by (A.22),
                 Z ν∗ (µ)
                          dν      4                                                    π
                             dµ = 2 arccosh(sinh 3µ) dµ = ρU (x) dx           at µ =     x.      (A.51)
                  −ν∗ (µ) πµ     π                                                     3

Zeros. By Theorem 7 the zeros of G in the strip, for any sequence that attains ΛV , follow the
one law ρU . The zeros of the symbol are not rigid in this sense: the argument of appendix A.2
fixes their limiting distribution only up to rearrangements inside K that leave the potential in
N unchanged, and appendix A.5 uses this freedom. The two statements concern the zeros of
different functions.

Three functionals. The functional of Theorem 5 places the zeros of G on the band, along the
Cardy line, where positivity is an inequality between two numbers that a computer certifies; it is

                                                   81
sharp to order c1/3 . The functional of section 9 places no zeros at all; it is one transform of one
real seed, and it stays below the Cardy line by a fixed margin. The functional of this appendix
places the zeros of the Mellin symbol on the creases; the constraint ĝ = −g is automatic, and
positivity comes from one real saddle point that dominates a conjugate pair by the margin η. In
the last two constructions the margin is bought in the same way, by moving the tip outward: the
deformation γ < π of section 9 borrows the extremal profile of a heavier theory, with c′ replaced
by (π/γ)2 c′ (9.13), and so does the dilation of (A.41), since c′ λ H∗ (ω/λ) is the potential of the
skeleton with c′ replaced by λc′ , at the same z. Neither gives a rate.

The problem on a strip. In the U (1)c problem the vacuum sits at the origin, on the boundary
of the half line of the Mellin transform, and the proof of [13, Ch. 1] works on a strip in the
Mellin variable, whose width is the dimension. Here the vacuum sits at t = −a, off the half
line. It cannot be reached by the transform, and enters only through the expansion (A.4) of its
S-image; the strip is replaced by the unknown set N , and the value of the slope is fixed by a
free-boundary condition. In the variable b the same vacuum is a boundary point of a fixed strip
(section 6.3), and no free boundary has to be found, although the largest useful slit is again
characterized by tangency (section 6.6).

only through the numerical values in the proofs. We write wα = 4π αt and t = c′ x2 /3 with
x ≥ 0, so that wa = c′ L(x), L(x) = 2π3 |x|.
