B.2    The weight
The weight V is a second difference of α 7→ Kα (t) with unit step, yet it is comparable to the
single kernel Ka : up to a polynomial factor as soon as t ≥ 4/c′ , and up to the factor 0.9 beyond
t = ξ1∗ c′ , where Ka−1 and Ka−2 are exponentially smaller than Ka . The proof writes V as an
average of ∂α2 Kα , a multiple of I2 , and compares Bessel functions at the nearby arguments wa ,
wa−1 , wa−2 through the logarithmic derivative I1 /I0 .

C     Potential theory on the slit strip
This appendix proves the lemmas of potential theory behind sections 6.5 to 6.8: the conformal
map and the harmonic measure of D, the entropy of that measure, the Poisson–Jensen and
Jensen inequalities on D, the edge bound, Proposition 6.4, and the last inequality of Lemma 6.7.
Appendix I uses Lemma C.4 and (C.15) again. From appendix B we take Lemmas B.1 to B.3.

whose value may change from line to line.

C.2     Entropy

we bound here. We write log+ t := max(log t, 0).

D     The zero law on the strip
This appendix proves the two statements of section 7 that tie the comb B to the extremal
potential U : the Riesz representation (Lemma 7.1; on the real axis it is the continuum limit of
the sums of appendix F) and the edge-phase lemma (Lemma 7.6; its sharper form Lemma D.2
is what verifies (SD2) in appendix E.4). A Poisson identity (Lemma D.1) links the two, and
appendix D.4 derives the eikonal identity of section 7.6.
    Throughout, b = x + iy, and b = v + 2i on E+ ; A ≲ B means A ≤ CB with C depending at
most on the width δ1 of appendix D.3. Besides U we use the complex potential of Lemma 6.5,

D.1    Riesz representation of U
Proof of Lemma 7.1. Plan. The superposition u of the kernels log|Tx′ | at density ρU has the
charge, the edge values and the growth of U ; so U − u is harmonic, and vanishes by Phragmén–
Lindelöf.                                                              ′)                  ′)

                                                                                         2     is

From Tx′ = (cosh πb − cosh πx )/(cosh πb + cosh πx ),
                              ′                    ′

    Uniqueness. The difference d := U − u satisfies ∆d = 0 in the sense of distributions, so
it is harmonic on S (Weyl’s lemma [63, §3.7]); it is continuous on S̄, vanishes on E± , and
|d(b)| ≤ C ′ (1 + |x|). For ϵ > 0 the function d − ϵ cosh 2x cos 2y is harmonic on S, is ≤ 0 on E±
because cos 1 > 0, and is negative on the cross-cuts |x| = X once X is large; by the maximum
principle it is ≤ 0 on S. Letting ϵ → 0, and repeating the argument with −d, we get d ≡ 0.

     In particular Φ1 , Φ′1 and Φ′′1 are bounded on Nδ1 uniformly in c′ .

Proof. Plan. Part (a) is the Poisson identity read along the edge, together with the Riesz
representation, which identifies Φ0 with U continued across E+ . Part (b) is Euler–Maclaurin
summation in the continuous site index. Part (c) bounds the terms of Φ1 one by one from the
decay (D.5) of the kernel and the behavior of N at the tip.
      (a). By (D.5) and ρU (x′ ) ≲ 1 + x′ , the integral (D.7) and its b-derivatives converge absolutely
and locally uniformly on Nδ1 , so Φ0 is analytic there. On E+ , Re φx′ = log|Tx′ | = 0 by (T1),
so Re Φ0 = 0; and φx′ ( 2i ) = 0 gives Φ0 ( 2i ) = 0. By (D.4), ∂b φx′ (v + 2i ) = −iπ[sech π(v − x′ ) +
sech π(v + x′ )], whence Φ′0 (v + 2i ) = − 4πi 3 F (v) by Lemma D.1. Next, Re Φ0 = U = Re U on
Nδ1 ∩ S by Lemma 7.1; this set does not meet the slits, as δ1 ≤ 14 , and two analytic functions with
equal real parts differ by an imaginary constant, so Φ0 = U + iγ0 and Φ′0 = − 4πi    3 p there: Φ0 is the
complex potential continued across E+ . This continues p analytically to Nδ1 , with p(w + 2i ) =
F (w) for |Im w| ≤ δ1 , which is (D.8). Differentiating, F ′ (w) = sinh πw (1 + cosh2 πw)−1/2 and
F ′′ (w) = 2π cosh πw (1 + cosh2 πw)−3/2 , as in (E.30), which for real w gives the sign statements.
In |Im w| ≤ 12 the only singularities of F are where cosh πw = ±i, on |Im w| = 12 ; so F ′′ and
F ′′′ are continuous on |Im w| ≤ δ1 , they tend to 0 as |Re w| → ∞, and C2 = 4π       3 sup|Im w|≤δ1 |F |,
                                                                                                       ′′

C3 = 4π  3 sup|Im w|≤δ1 |F | are finite.

E     Two-saddle steepest descent with a uniform remainder
This appendix proves Lemma 7.7: for band points x ≥ xI , ĥ0 (c′ x2 /3) is the sum Amp cos Ψ̂ of
two saddle-point contributions, up to a relative remainder εsd that is bounded explicitly and
tends to zero uniformly in x (Lemma E.1, Corollary E.2), and obeys a global bound that decays
in x (Corollary E.3). The comb enters only through the edge representation of Lemma 7.3(c)
and the edge phases Φ0 , Φ1 of Lemma 7.6, which we abstract into hypotheses (SD1)–(SD4) and
verify in appendix E.4.
                                                                                         ′
The idea. On E+ the integrand of the edge representation is e−c L(x) times an integrable
profile times a pure phase ei[Θ0 (v)+kx v] , where Θ0 (v) := c′ Im Φ0 (v + 2i ) has Θ′0 = − 4π
                                                                                             3 c F and
                                                                                                ′

kx = 4π3 c x. The phase is stationary at v = ±v0 (x), where F (v0 ) = x, with second derivative
          ′

∓c αx , αx = 4π
   ′
               3 F (v0 ). The two contributions are complex conjugates, and ordinary stationary
                  ′

phase gives their sum as Amp cos Ψ̂ with
                                                                           s
                1         iΨ̂(x)   2πc′           (1)                           2π
                2 Amp(x) e       =      h0 (b0 ) H0 (kx b0 ) b0 e−iπ/4               ,       b0 = v0 + 2i ,     (E.1)
                                    3                                          c′ αx

                                                             93
up to relative corrections that are O(1/c′ ) at fixed x. Toward the tip, however, the two points
coalesce at v = 0 and αx → 0, whereas section 7.7 needs the corrections small on all of [xI , ∞),
with xI → x∗1 as c → ∞. The proof therefore uses an explicit       
                                                                     contour: at height u above the
edge the oscillating factor has modulus exp 4π  3  c′ u[F (v) − x] up to a cubic error, so running

above the edge between the saddles and below it outside them gains a factor exponentially
small in c′ , at rates given by the chord slope sch and the curvature αx . At the saddle the
first-order terms are odd and cancel, leaving terms of order 1/(c′ αxk ), k = 1, 2, 3, with explicit
constants. Near the tip v0 , αx and sch vanish like (x − x∗1 )1/2 , and the remainder is governed by
c′ min{1, v0 }3 ≍ c′ (x − x∗1 )3/2 .

F     The comb on the real axis
This appendix proves the statements of section 7.5 about the comb on the real axis, namely
Proposition 7.4 and Lemma 7.5(a)–(c), (d1), (e), (f), and from them the floor of Lemma 7.9,
the overshoot bound of Lemma 7.11 and the first-cell bounds of Lemma 7.12; appendix G takes
appendices F.1 to F.4 and Lemmas F.8 to F.10 as input. Each statement carries its own tag, and
a statement that contains or uses a certified constant names the row of table 4 on which it rests.
    As in section 7.5, the logarithm of the comb is a sum over the sites of the site weight fx
of (F.1), a Riemann sum of unit step in the site index for the potential U = −L (Lemma 7.1).
On either side of x, apart from the sites far to the left of x, the summand is concave along the
sites, so the trapezoid and midpoint rules bound the sum by the integral with no remainder.
What is left over is explicit: the potential c′ P of the charge 2n1 below the first kept site, the
half cell Jend there, and the few sites nearest to x. Appendices F.1 to F.3 provide the tools,
appendix F.5 the two-sided bounds, and appendices F.4, F.6 and F.7 the consequences. The
constants that appendix G needs are explicit: CJ ≤ 0.65 (Lemma F.8), Cdet ≤ 16.6 (Lemma F.7),
and the coefficient 94 of n1 in (F.46).

Notation. Throughout, (m, k∞ , n1 , η0 ) = (1, 14 , 74 , 14 ), the outer factor is sech2 (πx/2), and

F.1      The law near the tip
We prove that the site weight is monotone and concave along the sites (Lemma F.1), expand the
law at the tip (Lemma F.2), and deduce the form of the comb in the Airy window, which gives
Lemma 7.5(b) and (d1). Near the tip the law is the square-root density of a soft edge: the sites
sit at ςk ≈ (k − 14 )2/3 , and the logarithm of each Blaschke factor is that of a scaled distance plus
log ℓc plus a constant. Rows 9 and 10 of table 4 are used.

Proof. (a) The derivatives follow from (log tanh z)′ = 2/ sinh 2z. For y > x both terms of
∂y fx are positive; for 0 < y < x the term with y − x is −π/ sinh π(x − y) and exceeds the
other in modulus, since x + y > x − y. This also gives |∂y fx | ≤ π/ sinh π(x − y) ≤ 1/(x − y),
while |∂y2 fx | ≥ π 2 cosh π(x − y)/ sinh2 π(x − y); the quotient is at least π coth π(x − y), and
coth z ≥ max{1, 1/z}.                                                                                      
    (b) With w = πy, ρ′U = 43 cosh w (sinh2 w − 1)−1/2 > 0 and dw                d
                                                                                    cosh w (sinh2 w − 1)−1/2 =
−2 sinh w (sinh2 w − 1)−3/2 < 0, so ρU is increasing and concave, and it vanishes at x∗1 . Fix
y = x∗1 +Rr and put ρ = ρU (y), s = ρ′U (y). The tangent at y lies above the graph, so
N (y) ≤ 12 0r [ρ − s(r − r′ )] dr′ = 12 (ρr − 12 sr2 ); and τ := sr/ρ ≤ 1 because the chord from the
tip is steeper than the tangent. Hence 4N s ≤ 2ρ2 τ (1 − τ /2) ≤ ρ2 , that is (log(ρ2U /N ))′ =
2ρ′U /ρU − ρU /(2N ) ≤ 0. The statements on χa follow from (F.2): χ′a > 0 and χ′′a < 0; a chord
of a concave function has a slope between the derivatives at its ends; the chord from a = 14 has
decreasing slope; and (F.9) is ρ2U (χa )/(a − 14 ) ≥ ρ2U (χa′ )/(a′ − 14 ) together with the monotonicity
of ρU .                                                                       
    (c) We have φ′ = ∂y fx χ′a and φ′′ = (χ′a )2 ∂y2 fx −∂y fx (log ρU )′ , both evaluated at y = χa . For
χa > x the bracket is negative by (a), since (log ρU )′ > 0. For χa < x we have φ′ < 0, and φ′′ ≤ 0
if and only if |∂y2 fx |/|∂y fx | ≥ (log ρU )′ (χa ); by (a) it suffices that (log ρU )′ (y) ≤ max{π, 1/(x−y)}
at y = χa . Let r := y − x∗1 . Row 9 of table 4 contains two facts: (log ρU )′ (x∗1 + r) ≤ 3.10 < π for
r ≥ 0.18 ⟨cert⟩, and 1.3 r (log ρU )′ (x∗1 + r) ≤ 0.95 for 0 < r ≤ 0.64 ⟨cert⟩. If r ≥ 0.18 the first fact
suffices. If r < 0.18, then y < max{x∗1 + 0.18, x − 1}, so y ≥ ythr (x) forces y ≥ x∗1 + (x − x∗1 )/2.3,
that is x − y ≤ 1.3 r, and the second fact gives (log ρU )′ (y) ≤ 0.95/(1.3r) ≤ 1/(x − y).

F.2     One-sided Euler–Maclaurin
Where the summands are concave along the sites, that is, except far to the left of x, the sums
in (F.3) are bounded on both sides by the integrals Ix with no remainder (Lemma F.5). Far to
the left of x the summands are smooth and small, and the ordinary midpoint remainder applies;
its total is the loss CL (Lemma F.6). Rows 5, 9 and 10 of table 4 are used.

F.3     Detuning costs O(η02 )
Lifting a double zero off the axis to the heights ±εk raises the modulus of its factor at a point
of the axis at a distance D ≫ εk by the relative amount O(ε2k /D2 ) only, and εk is η0 times the
local spacing. Thus lower bounds for B 0 are lower bounds for B, and upper bounds for B 0
become upper bounds for B at the cost of the two pairs which flank x, which are kept exactly,
                       2
and of a factor eCdet η0 with Cdet ≤ 16.6. No certified constant is used.

F.5    Two-sided bounds on the envelope, and the level
This subsection proves Lemma 7.5(a) and Proposition 7.4. We apply Lemma F.5 on either side
of x, but keep exactly the 2j + 2 sites nearest to x in the upper bound, and the 2j detuned pairs
nearest to x in the lower bound. The difference between these exact sites and the continuum
integral over their index range is the hole function. Sites and continuum carry the same total
charge, so all scales cancel, and as n → ∞ the hole function tends to an explicit function of the
position u in the cell alone. The rows used are 5, 6 and 9, and row 10 in the window clause of
Lemma F.8.

    Displacement: the sites. For i = 0, 1 we have p(i) = i, and nothing changes. For −j ≤ i ≤ −1,
u − p(i) ≥ u − i ≥ |i|, so 0 ≤ log(u − p(i)) − log(u − i) ≤ (i − p(i))/(u − i) ≤ (|i| + 1)/n by (F.49).
For 2 ≤ i ≤ j + 1, p(i) − u ≥ p(i) − p(1) ≥ e−1/2 (i − 1), so 0 ≤ log(i − u) − log(p(i) − u) ≤

between A/A0 and b/b0 . With A = (u − p(i))2 , A0 = (u − i)2 , b = η02 (ℓn+i /ℓn )2 , b0 = η02 , and

4(j + 1)/n; for i = 0, 1, where A = A0 , the same holds, and for η0 = 0 a pair factor is a squared
site factor. The sites i = 0, 1 do not move; the remaining weights in (F.41) add up to 4j − 2,
and there are 2j pairs in (F.42), so the sums over the sites change by at most 8j(j + 1)/n.

F.6    The detuned floor
We prove Lemma 7.9, the floor (F.53): on every cell n ≥ 2j + 4 the comb h0 stays above a
positive multiple of the level. The floor is the smallest value, over the position u in the cell, of

                                                   112
the exact contribution of the 2j detuned pairs nearest to x divided by that of the continuum
which they replace; all other pairs are bounded below by their undetuned values, and these by
the continuum through the midpoint rule. Keeping more pairs exactly removes a larger part of
the midpoint loss, which is why µ4 > µ1 . The rows used are 3 and 5, and row 9 through ythr .

F.7    The first cells
On the cells 2, . . . , 5 the point x is within a few Airy lengths of the tip, there are at most three
sites to its left, and nothing is gained by replacing them by a continuum. We therefore keep all
the sites up to χn+4 exactly and apply the midpoint rule only beyond them. No end term and
no loss eL arise, which is why the unit is Lev e−Jend . In the Airy variable the resulting finite
product is a c-independent function on each cell, and its minimum is certified (table 4, row 4;
row 10 for the passage to the Airy variable).

[proved, computer-assisted] This appendix proves Proposition 7.13: Band ≤ Rvac |Lobe|
with Rvac ≤ 0.1021 + 0.02, and |Lobe| → ∞; on the way it proves Lemma 7.5(d2). It is used
in section 7.9 only, and it takes Lemmas B.2 and B.4, Proposition 7.2, Lemma 7.3(b) (whose
hypothesis is verified in section 7.10), appendices F.1 to F.4, Lemma F.9, Lemma F.10 and
Lemma F.8 as input.
    The idea is described in section 7.9. The Cardy-weighted masses of the negative lobe of h0
below t⋆ and of the positive band beyond both come from a window of width O(ℓc ) at the tip,
                                                                                   4     1
and in the variable ς = (x − x∗1 )/ℓc each is a common factor, proportional to c′ 3 n1 − 6 = c′13/6 ,
times a c-independent integral. The ratio of the two integrals is certified to be at most 0.1021
when the band is cut after its first hundred cells; the finite-c corrections and the band beyond
these cells are bounded by hand, with explicit constants wherever a constant enters Rvac .

G.5      Why the Bessel asymptote must be two-sided; assembly
The lobe must be bounded from below and the band from above, and both carry the weight V ;
this is why the asymptote of Lemma B.4 has to be two-sided (section 7.9). With the one-sided
                    ′
bound V ≤ Ka ≤ ec L for the band (V ≤ Ka by the monotonicity of Kα in α, and Lemma B.2(e)),
and the true size V ≃ (1 − e−4πxq )2 as(c′ L) for the lobe, the two measures would differ at the

H     Certified constants and numerical methods
We say what a certified inequality is, list all of them in table 4, and complete the description of
the numerical methods of section 5.

H.1    What “certified” means
An inequality marked ⟨cert⟩ bounds an explicit elementary function of one real variable, or a
ratio of two integrals of such functions, or a quantity that the row reduces to finitely many
such functions (rows 5 and 8), on a stated range; it does not involve c. The inequality is
proved in interval arithmetic on a covering of the range by finitely many subintervals. A
real number is represented by an interval with floating-point endpoints that contains it, and
every operation returns an interval, rounded outward, that contains all values which the exact
result can take; rounding errors widen the result and cannot make it wrong. The function
is evaluated on each subinterval X as a whole, and the largest upper endpoint obtained is
a proven upper bound on the range (likewise for lower bounds); table 4 records it as the
bound obtained, which lies slightly beyond the true extremum. Where needed we use the
mean-value form f (X) ⊂ f (m) + f ′ (X) (X − m), with m the midpoint of X and f ′ (X) an
enclosure of the derivative on X; in the same form, the integral of f over X, of length h, lies in
h f (m) + 18 h2 [−w, w], with w the length of f ′ (X), and these enclosures are added (row 11). An
unbounded part of the range is bounded by hand, as the row records. This, together with the
legend of section 1.3, is the only meaning of “computer-assisted” in this paper.
     A point at which the function is singular, or is a quotient of two vanishing quantities, is
removed analytically first. In row 4 the simple zero at the left end of cell 2 is divided out. In row 6
the terms 2 log u+2 log(1−u) of h±                  2

      We use the Arb library [61] through its Python interface python-flint. The precision is 80,
100, 128 or 200 bits, as the row says. Each computation has been checked independently, line
by line, against the formulas as they are printed in this paper. Rows 3, 6 and 8 have also been
certified a second time, by a computation written independently (row 6 through log Γ and the
digamma and trigamma functions).
      Table 4 is a closed list: a c-independent constant carries the mark ⟨cert⟩ in section 7
and appendices F and G only if it has a row. Constants obtained by hand within a proof, and
the orientation values of section 7.3, are not certified and are not listed. Absent by choice are the
absolute constants of the c-dependent remainders, such as the Hankel remainder of appendix B
and the steepest-descent constants of appendix E; they stay symbolic and enter only through
catt
  0 . All rows feed Theorem 5, hence the attainment half of Theorem 1, and Corollary 6; no row
feeds Theorems 2 to 4 or Theorem 7.

H.2     Numerical methods for the optimal functionals
[numerical] We complete section 5.1; nothing here is certified. At order P the functional is
the unique p(t)e−2πt , with p in the truncated basis of [11], that has a simple zero at t1 and
double zeros at t2 , . . . , tP/2 . We scale the rows of the truncated crossing equations by the
vacuum functional. A Newton step is accepted or damped by a monotonicity test on successive
corrections, not by the size of the residual, which is a poor guide at large c.
    Precision. Arb serves here for its arbitrary precision and not for its error bounds: the
radii grow through the Laguerre recurrence until they carry no information. We work with the
midpoints and establish the accuracy by solving again at 1.5 to 2 times the precision. On the
levels that were solved twice, at least 31 digits agree in t1 , and at least 20 in every level and
weight that we report; the highest orders, P ≥ 2304, are judged by the Newton residual instead.
The working precision is 768–1600 bits on most levels, and at least one bit per unit of P at the
highest orders.
                                                (P )                      s
    Extrapolation in P . For each c we fit ∆1 (c) = ∆∞ + A e−k(P/c) , with s free, to the levels
                                                                                          (∞)
with P/c ≥ 0.8; the fits return s = 0.57–0.58. The fitted ∆∞ is our estimate S of ∆1 (c). Its
uncertainty is σ := 12 max{|S − T |, |S − S1/2 |}, with a floor of 10−7 c, where S1/2 is the same fit
with s = 12 fixed, and T is a fit in which the dependence on P/c is taken from a fully converged
                                                                     (P    )
chain, c = 48. When Pmax /c ≥ 6 we subtract instead from ∆1 max (c) a tail fitted, with s free,
to the top levels (at most six), and σ is the spread of the fits with s = 12 , s = 1 and s free. For
Pmax /c ≥ 4 the whole correction is at most 3 · 10−6 c. We tested the estimator on ten chains
with 64 ≤ c ≤ 512 that reach P/c ≥ 5, by cutting each at a smaller P/c and extrapolating again.
When the cut is at P/c ≥ 2, the error is below 10−5 c and below σ in every case. When the cut

I       Limit profiles: proofs for section 10
This appendix proves the statements of section 10: three inequalities at finite c, which carry
all the input from section 6 (appendix I.1); Lemma 10.1, which is a quotation (appendix I.2);
Lemma 10.2 and Proposition 10.3 (appendix I.3); and Theorem 7 (appendix I.4). Beyond
appendix I.1 only the potential theory of subharmonic functions in the plane is used [63, 72].
    Throughout, the notation is that of section 10.1, dA is the area measure in the b-plane, and
U is the extremal potential extended across the slits. We use these properties of U (Lemma 6.5,
Definition 6.6, Lemma 6.7 and (C.15)): it is continuous on S and harmonic on D; U ≤ 0; U = −L
                                                                                  1
on the slits and U < −L on (−x∗1 , x∗1 ); and it is subharmonic on S, its charge 2π ∇2 U being the
measure ρU (x) dx ⊗ δy=0 on the slits |x| ≥ x∗1 , with the density ρU of (6.21).

I.2    Proof of Lemma 10.1
We check that the profiles are uniformly bounded above, quote the compactness theorem for
subharmonic functions, and pass from potentials to charges by integrating by parts.
    The theorem quoted [72, Thms. 3.2.12–3.2.13]. Let X ⊂ R2 be open and connected, and
let uj ̸≡ −∞ be subharmonic functions on X, bounded above on every compact subset of X
uniformly in j. Then either uj → −∞ uniformly on every compact subset of X, or a subsequence
converges in L1loc (X). If uj → u in L1loc (X), then u is almost everywhere equal to a subharmonic
function, again called u, and for every compact K ⊂ X and every continuous f : K → R

                                 lim sup sup (uj − f ) ≤ sup (u − f ).                               (I.1)
                                   j→∞     K                   K

Proof of Lemma 10.1. Each uj is subharmonic on S and not identically −∞, since Gj is holo-
morphic and not identically zero, and, for j so large that cj ≥ 40, supS uj ≤ (2 log c′j + C)/c′j
by Lemma I.1(a). So the uj are uniformly bounded above from some j on, and the theo-
rem applies with X = S, which is connected. Either (i) holds, or there is a subsequence
with uj → u in L1loc (S) and u subharmonic; u ̸≡ −∞ because u is locally integrable. For
                                          1
the
R
    charges write nj := ngj andR nu := 2π   ∇2 u.R For a smooth φ of compact support in S,
           1 R     2          1      2
  φ dnj = 2π uj ∇ φ dA → 2π u ∇ φ dA = φ dnu . Choosing φ ≥ 0 with φ ≥ 1 on a
compact K shows that supj nj (K) < ∞, and then the convergence extends from smooth to
continuous φ of compact support by uniform approximation: nj → nu vaguely.

   We record the standard consequence that is used for Theorem 7(b); it follows by testing
against continuous functions that decrease to 1K and, for the second statement, also against

I.3    Proofs of Lemma 10.2 and Proposition 10.3
For the majorant we pass Lemma I.1(b) to the limit almost everywhere and remove the exceptional
set by averaging over small disks. For the contact point we follow the points at which Lemma I.1(c)
holds and use (I.1): a limit cannot lose what the uj have at nearby points.

                                                     124
Proof of Lemma 10.2. Let uj → u in L1loc (S). A subsequence converges almost everywhere, so
Lemma I.1(b) gives u ≤ U almost everywhere on every compact Q ⊂ D. Hence u ≤ U almost
everywhere on S, because the slits have zero area. For
                                                    R
                                                        b ∈ S and smallR r the sub-mean-value
property of u on the disk B(b, r) gives u(b) ≤ πr1 2 B(b,r) u dA ≤ πr1 2 B(b,r) U dA → U (b) as
r → 0, since U is continuous on S. The rest follows from U ≤ 0, U = −L on the slits and
U < −L on (−x∗1 , x∗1 ).

Proof of Proposition 10.3. By Lemma I.1(c) with xmax = x∞ + 1, for large j there are           
                                                                                                  points
xj ∈ [0, x1 (gj )] with uj (xj ) + L(xj ) ≥ −ϵj , where ϵj := log c′j + log(32π 2 (x∞ + 1)4 /3) /c′j → 0.
Let K := [0, x∞ +1], which contains xj for large j. If alternative (i) held, then uj (xj ) ≤ supK uj →
−∞, in contradiction with uj (xj ) ≥ −L(xj ) − ϵj ≥ − 2π    3 (x∞ + 1) − 1. So (i) does not occur. Let
u be a limit profile. Passing to a further subsequence, uj → u in L1loc (S) and xj → x0 ∈ [0, x∞ ].
Fix r ∈ (0, 12 ) and apply (I.1) with Kr := B̄(x0 , r) and f (b) := −L(Re b), which we write −L.
Since xj ∈ Kr for large j, supKr (u + L) ≥ lim supj supKr (uj + L) ≥ lim supj uj (xj ) + L(xj ) ≥ 0.
As r ↓ 0 the left side decreases to u(x0 ) + L(x0 ), because u + L is upper semicontinuous. With
Lemma 10.2, −L(x0 ) ≤ u(x0 ) ≤ U (x0 ) ≤ −L(x0 ), so all three are equal. Finally U (x0 ) = −L(x0 )
fails for 0 ≤ x0 < x∗1 by Lemma 6.7, so x0 ≥ x∗1 .

I.4    Proof of Theorem 7
We reduce the theorem to the claim that every limit profile u of the sequence is equal to U ,
and prove the claim for the deficit θ := U − u in three steps: a dichotomy on the slit strip D
(Step 1), the exclusion of θ > 0 by two incompatible bounds on the means of θ over small circles
about the tip (Step 2), and the points of the slits (Step 3).
    Reduction. By Proposition 10.3 with x∞ = x∗1 , every subsequence has a further subsequence
that converges in L1loc (S) to a limit profile u whose contact point lies in [x∗1 , x∗1 ], that is, u(x∗1 ) =
U (x∗1 ). Suppose that every such u is equal to U . Since convergence in L1loc (S) is convergence in
a metric, the whole sequence then converges to U , which is (a). The integration by parts in the
                                                                               1
proof of Lemma 10.1, applied to the whole sequence, then gives nj → 2π            ∇2 U = ρU dx ⊗ δy=0
vaguely, which is (10.2). This measure gives no mass to a compact set disjoint from the slits,
and none to the boundary of a rectangle [xa , xb ] × [−y0 , y0 ], since it has a density on the real
axis. So (I.2) gives the two counting statements of (b), after multiplication by c′j . For (c), the
map b 7→ c′j b2 /3 is injective and holomorphic on Re b > 0, and Gj (b) = gj (c′j b2 /3), so the zeros
of Gj in the rectangle and the zeros of gj in its image correspond, with multiplicities. The
                     c′
image of x + iy is 3j (x2 − y 2 ) + 2i                    2          3
                                    3 cj xy. With ξ = x /3, dx = 2x dξ and ρV = 3ρU /4x we have
                                        ′
R xb              R ξb
  xa ρU (x) dx = ξa 2ρV (ξ) dξ.
     It remains to prove the claim. Let u be a limit profile with u(x∗1 ) = U (x∗1 ), and θ := U − u on
S. By Lemma 10.2, θ ≥ 0. It is lower semicontinuous (−θ is upper semicontinuous: at a point θ
can jump down, never up), with values in [0, +∞], it is not identically +∞, and θ(x∗1 ) = 0. On D
it is superharmonic, because there U is harmonic. Let r0 := x∗1 /2, so that the disk B := B(x∗1 , r0 )
lies in S and does not meet the left slit.
     Step 1: dichotomy. Since D is connected, the strong minimum principle [63, Thm. 2.3.1]
says that a non-negative superharmonic function on D that vanishes at one point of D vanishes
identically. So either θ ≡ 0 on D, or θ > 0 at every point of D.
     Step 2: θ > 0 on D is impossible. Suppose that θ > 0 on D. Let Ω0 := B \ [x∗1 , x∗1 + r0 ), a
simply connected subdomain of D, and let A := {x∗1 + r0 eiϕ : π4 ≤ ϕ ≤ 3π     4 }, a compact arc in D.
Since θ is lower semicontinuous
                    p
                                   and    positive on A, θ A := min(1, min A θ) lies in (0, 1]. The local
coordinate w := b − x1 , with arg(b − x1 ) ∈ (0, 2π), maps Ω0 conformally onto the half-disk
                          ∗                    ∗

H0 := {|w| < R, Im w > 0}, R := r0 . It extends continuously to the boundary, maps the two
lips of the slit and the tip to the diameter [−R, R], and maps A to A′ := {Reiϕ : π8 ≤ ϕ ≤ 3π         8 }.

because |Re−iϕ − w|2 − |Reiϕ − w|2 = 4R sin ϕ Im w ≥ 0, and its first term alone gives the
Poisson integral of an indicator function. It tends to 0 at every boundary point of H0 outside
A′ . Let h(b) := θA ω(w(b)) on Ω0 ; it is θA times the harmonic measure of A in Ω0 . Now θ − h
is superharmonic on Ω0 , and its lower limit at every boundary point of Ω0 is non-negative: at
points of A because θ ≥ θA ≥ h and θ is lower semicontinuous; elsewhere because θ ≥ 0 and
h → 0, the two lips of the slit being approached separately. By the minimum principle ([63,
Thm. 2.3.1] applied to h − θ on the bounded domain Ω0 ), θ ≥ h on Ω0 .

   Step 3: the slits. We have u = U on D. At a point x of a slit, upper semicontinuity of u
and continuity of U give u(x) ≥ lim supb→x, b∈D u(b) = U (x), and u(x) ≤ U (x) by Lemma 10.2.
Hence u = U on all of S, which is the claim. This completes the proof of Theorem 7.

Letters with two meanings. j: pairs kept (§7, App. F), sequence index (§10, App. I). δ: distance below the
tip (§6, App. C), above it in the variable πx (§F.1). r: Abel ratio (§8), distance from a tip (Lem. 7.5). y: Im τ ,
the height of a sheet √
                      (§3, §4), Im b (elsewhere). E: boundary set, edges E± , factor E(w). a, u, σ: also a real
label in χa , and 4πt, 4πt in §8. Distinct: W (ρ), Wq,ℓ ; eβ , eL , e1 (δ); ω, ωb ; κ(δ), κtip , κ. Bare ε, ϵ: local to a
statement; η: Dedekind function (§2.1 only); ϱ: a ratio, not a density; fraktur letters: measures.
Asymptotic relations. A ≃ B: A/B → 1 in the limit indicated. A ≍ B in a proof (§7, the appendices):
C −1 B ≤ A ≤ CB with a constant C ≥ 1 that does not depend on c or on the point x; it may depend on the fixed
parameters of the construction, and on a parameter that is named at that place. A ≲ B: A ≤ CB with such a C
(in App. D it may depend on δ1 ); A ≳ B: B ≲ A. In §3 and §4, ≍ keeps the exponential order in c only. With
explicit numbers on the right (c′ ≲ 414), ≲ reads “at most, approximately”.
Status tags (legend at the end of §1.3). [proved], [proved, computer-assisted], [conjecture], [heuristic],
[numerical], [orientation], on a statement or a section; ⟨cert⟩ marks a certified inequality, a row of Table 4.
Terminology. Gap: ∆1 only; two sites bound a cell. BTZ threshold: t = 0 only; a functional certifies a gap bound.
Feasible at T , weakly feasible: Def. 2.3. Lobe interval [0, t1 ), with the negative lobe of g; band: beyond t1 (§2.5).
Envelope: |g|env of an oscillating |g|, and Env; U is a majorant of profiles. Edges: E± only; the slits end at tips.
Onset: near the first kept site χ2 (§7.1); onset index: n1 . Optimality, attainment: the halves of Thm. 1 (§2.6).
